Title: Computing with qLDPC Codes by Climbing the Chain Map Hierarchy

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.02999v1

Published Time: Fri, 04 Sep 2026 00:04:19 GMT

Number of Pages: 68

Markdown Content:
# Computing with qLDPC Codes by Climbing the Chain Map Hierarchy 

Rahul Sahay ∗,1, † David M. Long ∗,2, ‡ and Vedika Khemani 2, §

> 1

Department of Physics, Harvard University, Cambridge, Massachusetts 02138, USA 

> 2

Department of Physics, Stanford University, Stanford, California 94305, USA 

We develop a framework for logical computation with qLDPC codes that places logical Pauli, Clifford, and non-Clifford operations on the same footing. This brings the simple homological de-scription of Pauli logicals to the patchwork landscape of logical Clifford and non-Clifford operations, providing a tool for the discovery of new logical gates. In particular, we define the chain map hierarchy : a family of chain complexes whose homology classes encode logical unitary and code surgery operations at any level of the Clifford hierarchy, precisely analogous to the chain complex description of Pauli logicals. Consequently, intuition for Pauli logicals can be leveraged to discover new logical operations on qLDPC codes. For instance, the familiar ability to deform Pauli logicals with stabilizers—i.e. boundaries of the chain complex—becomes a way to search for constant-depth unitary implementations of (non-)Clifford logical gates. Using this strategy, we discover constant-depth unitary implementations of the full logical Clifford group on any number of blocks of the 2D toric code, including within a single block, and addressable logical CCZ gates on any triple of logical qubits on any number of blocks of the 3D toric code. Beyond manifold codes, we find address-able non-Clifford gates on codes with many encoded qubits. The chain map hierarchy naturally encompasses and extends other constructions of logical gadgets, for instance providing a univer-sal parameterization of cup products. As such, our work provides a unified, useful, and intuitive language for computing with qLDPC codes. 

I. Introduction 

Determining how to reliably store and process quan-tum information in physical systems composed of unreli-able components [1] is one of the most conceptually rich and technologically pressing fronts in physics. In this effort, much has been understood with regards to how to store information—that is, in developing schemes for a robust quantum memory [2, 3]. As such, the critical next step is to determine: How do we manipulate stored information? 

This is especially important given recent advances in quantum low-density parity check (qLDPC) codes, the best of which store many logical qubits into a single code block [3–12]. Ultimately, for these logical qubits to be useful for computation, we need to be able to individually and arbitrarily manipulate them with the same fluency with which we control their constituent physical qubits. However, even for simple qLDPC codes, we understand surprisingly little. Forgetting more complicated codes such as those that store tens or hundreds of logical qubits, there are substantial gaps in our understanding even for the 2D toric code [13], which has just two encoded qubits. For instance, with current approaches [14, 15], simply performing a logical Hadamard on one of the two qubits involves a complex architecture of ancillas scaffolding the code [16]. This is indicative of the state of the art. The current approach is frequently to construct examples of codes     

> ∗Equal contribution.
> †rsahay@g.harvard.edu
> ‡dmlong@stanford.edu
> §vkhemani@stanford.edu

or logical gadgets that meet a given target—e.g. gad-gets at a particular level of the Clifford hierarchy or with a given spacetime overhead—rather than to understand the principles and structures underlying logical compu-tation. Ideally, we would understand the landscape of all 

codes and the logical operations that can be performed with them. Without such a holistic framework, gaps in our understanding are left behind that rapidly compound to impede future progress. Indeed, finding more princi-pled approaches to fault-tolerant computation in qLDPC codes is increasingly the focus of recent work [17–28]. It is therefore useful to take a step back and ask: How is it that we have made so much progress on understand-ing quantum memory? A conceptual approach for under-standing quantum memories originated from the afore-mentioned 2D toric code, a topologically ordered system whose properties are derived from the homology of the 2D torus [13]. By generalizing the toric code, it was realized that any Calderbank-Shor-Steane (CSS) code [29, 30] can be understood through a homological formalism based on the mathematical structure of a chain complex [13, 31– 33]. The chain complex formalism has been a cornerstone of progress on quantum memory. It repackages the stabi-lizer description of quantum codes to mirror the structure of the toric code. Importantly, the chain complex pro-vides a full characterization of the code’s Pauli logicals: homology classes label distinct logical operators, while boundaries label stabilizers that relate different physical representatives of the same logical. Key properties of the codes, such as the code’s rate and distance, are de-rived from this homological description. Moreover, pow-erful tools from homological algebra—notably homolog-ical products [6, 8–10, 34, 35]—are responsible for both the best finite instances [36–40], and asymptotic fami-arXiv:2609.02999v1 [quant-ph] 2 Sep 2026 2

FIG. 1. Computation with qLDPC Codes and the Chain Map Hierarchy. The relationship between Pauli logical gates and the homology of a chain complex D• (top row) is extended to Clifford gates (middle row) and non-Clifford gates up the Clifford hierarchy (bottom row). In particular, (left column) certain logical operations—both unitary and measurement—from the ℓth level of the Clifford hierarchy (illustrated here for Cℓ−1Z gates) are encoded in the homology classes of (right column) an auxiliary chain complex [ C[ℓ]]. We call the collection of these auxiliary chain complexes the chain map hierarchy . (Middle row) Concretely, physical Clifford gates between codes C• and D• are encoded in [ C[2] ], which is isomorphic to the tensor product [C[2] ] ≃ D• ⊗ C•. For instance, a tensor like d ⊗ c encodes a CZ gate between qubits at c ∈ C• and d ∈ D•, and is thought of as a map c 7 → d. Logical Clifford gates are encoded as homologically non-trivial cycles of this chain complex, and can be deformed by boundaries corresponding to logically trivial Clifford stabilizers . (Bottom row) Higher levels of the hierarchy are iteratively defined as [ C[3] ] ≃ D• ⊗ C• ⊗ B•, and so on. A tensor like d ⊗ c ⊗ b encodes, for instance, a CCZ , and is thought of as a map from b to the CZ encoded by d ⊗ c.

lies [8–12, 41] of qLDPC codes. In our work, we generalize the chain complex descrip-tion of quantum codes—i.e. memory—to an organizing principle for computation with any qLDPC code. We define auxiliary chain complexes that, analogous to the Pauli case, provide a full characterization of the code’s Clifford and non-Clifford logical gates. Each logical ac-tion is labeled by a distinct homology class, with repre-sentative cycles encoding physical gate implementations. Strikingly, the chain complex boundaries furnish a notion of ( non-)Clifford stabilizers that relate distinct physical implementations of the same logical action. These sta-bilizer deformations can be used to find constant-depth implementations of logical actions (when they exist). For instance, returning to the 2D toric code, we use this ap-proach to find a logical unitary gate that performs a Hadamard on one of its two qubits, circumventing the need for a complex ancilla architecture. Indeed, our ap-proach reveals constant-depth unitary implementations of the entire Clifford group in the toric code. Our formalism encompasses and extends most known constructions of logical unitary gates, and even extends to measurement gadgets. This includes Clifford gadgets (e.g. code surgery [14–16, 18, 19, 42–53], fold-transversal gates [54–56], homomorphic transversal gates [17, 57], etc.) and constructions of non-Clifford gadgets (e.g. those based on cup products [20–26, 58, 59] and recently introduced non-Abelian code surgery gadgets [60–65]). Consequently, our work presents a unified and useful framework for thinking about computation in qLDPC codes. 

A. Summary of Results 

(1) The Chain Map Hierarchy: We develop a chain complex formalism that places Pauli, Clifford, and non-Clifford operations on the same footing. 3In the conventional chain complex formalism, physical 

Pauli operators are associated with chains in the com-plex, while logical Paulis correspond to cycles [Fig. 1, top row]. Physical Clifford gates (e.g. CZ gates) acting be-tween two codes map Paulis of one to Paulis of the other, and are thus encoded as linear maps between the code’s (co)chain complexes C• and D•. We show that these maps can themselves be organized as the chains of a chain complex [ C[2] ] [Fig. 1 (middle row, right)], whose cycles are chain maps —maps with well-defined homological ac-tion. The homology classes of [ C[2] ] hence label distinct 

logical Clifford actions between these codes, and their representative cycles encode the full space of physical im-plementations of the same logical action. The aforemen-tioned Clifford stabilizers are precisely the boundaries of [C[2] ] that deform these cycles/physical implementations. The chain complex [ C[2] ] is simply the tensor product [C[2] ] ≃ C• ⊗ D• (closely related to the hypergraph prod-uct), analogous to how the space of maps between vector spaces V and W is isomorphic to W ⊗ V ∗. Hence, logical Clifford gates between C• and D• are in correspondence with logical Pauli gates of an auxiliary code given by their tensor product! This construction iteratively extends to gates at any level ℓ of the Clifford hierarchy [66]. 1 For instance, non-Clifford gates at level ℓ = 3 (e.g. CCZ ) map Pauli oper-ators in a code B• to Clifford operators between codes 

C• and D•. Hence, these gates are captured by a chain complex of maps between B• and [ C[2] ], which we dub [C[3] ] ≡ D• ⊗ C• ⊗ B• [Fig. 1 (bottom row)]. This defines a collection of chain complexes encoding gates at increas-ing levels of the Clifford hierarchy, called the the chain map hierarchy . These complexes characterize inter-block unitary logical gates of CℓZ and CℓX type, certain intra-block gates (e.g. S gates), and even logical measurement (code surgery) gadgets up the Clifford hierarchy. 

(2) Newly Discovered Transversal Gates: The ho-mology classes of the chain map hierarchy characterize the space of physical implementations of logical Clif-ford and non-Clifford gates. We explicitly demonstrate how this can be used to find transversal —i.e. constant depth 2—unitary gates in qLDPC codes, e.g.: 

• The Full Clifford Group in the 2D Toric Code: 

[Fig. 2(a)] We find implementations of the entire Clifford group on any number of copies of the toric code (including a single copy). This includes new addressable CZ and CNOT gates acting between the two logical qubits of a single toric code and new

> 2Some authors use transversal to mean strictly depth one, or make a distinction between geometrically local and non-local (“fold transversal” [55]) gates. We say a circuit is transversal if it is constant depth with (possibly non-local) few-body gates.

FIG. 2. Applications. The chain map hierarchy can be used to discover new transversal logical gates. For instance, we use it to discover (a) the full Clifford group on the 2D toric code, (b) new addressable non-Clifford gates in the 3D toric code, and (c) addressable Clifford and non-Clifford gates in families of fracton codes with many encoded logical qubits. (d) The chain map hierarchy also unifies several other approaches to finding transversal gates, including cup product constructions and code surgery gadgets for logical measurement. 

addressable S and Hadamard gates each acting on only one of the qubits of the toric code. 

• Addressable Non-Clifford Gates in the 3D Toric Code: [Fig. 2(b)] We discover addressable CCZ 

gates that can act on any triple of logical qubits in any number of copies of the 3D toric code, includ-ing an intra-block logical CCZ gate acting within a single copy. 

• Addressable Gates in Non-Manifold Codes: 

[Fig. 2(c)] We find addressable 3 Clifford and non-Clifford gates in families of fracton models whose number of encoded logical qubits diverge with system size. Our formalism can also be used to assess when a logi-cal gate is impossible to implement transversally. While a fuller treatment is left to future work, we make an in-formal generalization of the Bravyi-K¨ onig theorem [67] to non-local circuits.      

> 3For codes with a number of logical qubits that diverges with in-creasing system size, “addressable” means that a gate acts on an
> O(1) logical qubits. For codes with a constant number of logical qubits (e.g. the toric code), addressable is taken to mean that the gate acts on the minimal number of logical qubits possible (i.e. a logical CZ must act on at least two logical qubits, a logical
> Smust act on at least one logical qubit, etc.).

4

(3) Unifying Other Perspectives on Computing with qLDPC Codes: Our formalism encompasses and extends to other approaches to computation and to finding logical operations on qLDPC codes. In par-ticular, logical unitary gates and measurement (code surgery) gadgets appear as different homology classes of the complexes in the chain map hierarchy. More-over, approaches to finding transversal gates—e.g. via cup products [21, 22, 24]—can be understood and gen-eralized within our formalism. In particular, we use the chain map hierarchy to develop a universal parameteri-zation of both the space of cup products and cohomology invariants obtained from cup products. This enables us to find, for instance, cup products in qLDPC codes that live outside of any prior parameterizations of the cup product present in literature. The paper is organized as follows. Section II provides a minimal and intuitive presentation of some of the key ideas of our work. Section III then precisely defines and characterizes the chain map hierarchy. Section IV ap-plies this formalism to discover new transversal gates in several well known qLDPC codes, including the full Clifford group in the 2D toric code. Section V explains how prior approaches to constructing gates—namely cup products—fit within our framework. We conclude with discussion in Section VI, which notably includes an in-formal derivation of a generalization of the Bravyi-K¨ onig theorem [67] to transversal circuits with non-local con-nectivity.

> II. Overview of Key Ideas

Before presenting a complete treatment of our results, we provide an intuitive overview of some key ideas and applications of our work. This is not intended to be ex-haustive and several results of interest are left for later sections—e.g. intra-block gates, relations to cup prod-ucts and code surgery, and several explicit gate construc-tions. The bulk of Section II A describes how physical imple-mentations of logical Clifford gates on two CSS codes are directly encoded in the Pauli logicals of an auxiliary code given by their tensor product. We illustrate this idea concretely in the case of the 2D toric code. In particular, we show that the physical implementation of logical CZ 

gates on the 2D toric code can be “read-off” from the Pauli logicals of the 4D toric code [explicitly see Fig. 3 and Section II A 4]. We then naturally generalize this idea to handle gates up the Clifford hierarchy, whose im-plementations are encoded in a family of auxiliary codes called the chain map hierarchy. Section II B then highlights a key application of these insights. We primarily discuss how deformations of the logical Pauli operators of the auxiliary code map back to deformations of the physical implementation of a logical (non-)Clifford gate. This can then be used as a tool for finding constant-depth implementations of logical gates up the Clifford hierarchy. 5 

> A. Unifying Logical Pauli and (Non-)Clifford Transversal Gates

Key Ideas. The key ideas of this section are: 1. Logical Cliffords can be deformed by local Clif-ford stabilizers . [Section II A 2] 2. Certain Clifford gates can be organized into a chain complex called the chain map complex 

[C[2] ], whose homology labels logical Clifford gates and whose boundaries encode Clifford stabilizers [Section II A 3]. 3. If the Cliffords act between codes described by complexes C• and D•, then [ C[2] ] ≃ D• ⊗ C•.Logical Cliffords can hence be “read off’ from the Pauli logicals of an auxilliary hypergraph product code [Fig. 3 and Section II A 4]. 4. Each of the above iteratively extends to certain gates up the Clifford hierarchy [Section II A 5]. We demonstrate these in the context of the toric code, a CSS stabilizer code defined on a square lattice tiling of a torus. As a CSS code, the toric code’s stabilizers, logical Pauli operators, and syndromes under the action of Pauli operators can be understood through the mathematical concept of a chain complex, C•, and its dual cochain complex, C• (see Section III A for a detailed review) [13, 31, 32, 34, 68]: 

(1) Both C• and C• are sequences of binary vector spaces connected by matrices, called boundary maps ( ∂j ) and coboundary maps (d j = ∂T 

> j

), respectively. Physically, C•

organizes the Z-type Pauli data: C1 labels the Z sta-bilizers, C0 labels physical Z operators, and C−1 labels how these operators can violate the code space. Dually, 

C• organizes the corresponding X-type Pauli data, with 

C−1, C0, and C1 labeling X stabilizers, physical X op-erators, and their syndromes. For the toric code, the spaces are in correspondence with the plaquettes, edges, and vertices of the lattice, with the (co)boundary maps encoding their incidence relations. In a bit more detail, the basis vectors of C0 or C0 are in correspondence with the qubits comprising the CSS code. Vectors in these spaces are called 0-chains and 0-cochains and they describe products of Z and X operators at the qubit locations where the vectors are non-zero. A similar idea is true for C1 and C−1 whose basis vectors label a basis of Z and X stabilizers that generate the stabilizer group. The boundary maps ∂1 : C1 → C0 and d 0 :

C−1 → C0 describe how this basis maps into physical products of Z and X operators. Finally, the basis vectors of C−1 and C1 encode the elementary syndromes of the code; the boundary maps ∂0 : C0 → C−1 and d 1 : C0 →

C1 encode the syndromes created by products of Z and 

X operators. The fact that stabilizers don’t violate the code space is encoded in the fact that the (co)boundary maps satisfy the characteristic property that ∂j ·∂j+1 = 0 (d j · dj−1 = 0)—i.e. the boundary of a boundary is zero. We further note that the Pauli X and Z logical gates are labeled by the the (co)homology groups H0 ≃

ker (∂0)/im (∂1) and H0 ≃ ker (d 1)/im (d 0). This is because such operators do not violate the code space—i.e. live in 

ker (∂0) and ker (d 1)—and two such operators are treated as equivalent if they can be deformed into each other by products of stabilizers—i.e. im (∂1) and im (d 0).  

> 1. Logical Gates as Chain Maps

We will soon demonstrate that Clifford and non-Clifford operators between codes also form a chain com-plex, whose zeroth homology group labels logical (non-) Clifford gates. We begin by recalling that logical Clif-ford gates are conventionally described as maps between chain complexes [17]. This is natural from the defining property of a Clifford unitary U , which maps every Pauli operator P to another Pauli operator P ′ under conjuga-tion: U P U † = P ′.As a concrete example, consider two copies of the toric code, which we will depict in orange and blue, with a transversal Clifford CZ gate between them: 

(2) Here, each black line indicates a physical CZ gate, and the action of this gate on stabilizers is shown on the right. Under conjugation, a two-qubit CZ gate leaves Z un-changed and maps an X on either qubit to itself times a Z

on the other. Thus, conjugation by the CZ ’s above takes 

X → XZ , so each X acquires a Z acting a half diagonal translation backwards, and X → XZ , with the Z act-ing a half diagonal translation forwards. Consequently, each X stabilizer maps to itself times a half-translated 6

Z stabilizer in the other code. Since the stabilizer group is preserved, the circuit preserves the joint code space and implements a logical Clifford gate. We can use these rules to derive how the logical Pauli operators transform:

logical of the other. Since X-type Pauli operators are represented by cochains and Z-type Pauli operators by chains [20], the additional Z acquired by an X under conjugation with

φ0 : C0 → C0 specifies the pattern of physical CZ gates: given an edge e labeling a qubit in the orange code, φ0(e)is the set of edges (qubits) in the blue code coupled to it with CZ gates; in Eq. (2), this is the qubit in the blue code displaced by half a diagonal backwards. Provided that the matrices in φ• are sparse, the gate above is im-plemented in constant depth. To ensure the gates in Eq. (4) preserve the code space and hence define a logical gate, we require that the chain map sends stabilizers to stabilizers, i.e. boundaries to (co)boundaries. Formally, this is ensured when the di-agram in Eq. 4 commutes , i.e. 

φj ◦ dj + ∂−j+1 ◦ φj−1 = 0 (5) for all j. This is the defining condition for φ• to be a 

chain map , reviewed in detail in Sec. III A 1. We remark that, while we introduce chain maps in the context of logical CZ gates, they can also describe gates related to 

CZ by the Hadamards on either qubit (e.g. CNOT ) by re-placing chains with cochains, or vice versa (Sec. III B 4). Furthermore, we later show how these chain maps can be used to describe gates acting within a single code block (c.f. Section III D). The chain map perspective on Clifford gates is natural but, as described up to this point, has two important shortcomings. First, it is apparently not clear how to organize the space of logical Clifford circuits. This is in contrast with logical Paulis, which can be systematically found by computing the homology of the chain complex. Second, it is not clear whether a logical Clifford gate can be modified while preserving its logical action—again in contrast to Pauli gates. We now fill in both of these gaps.  

> 2. Local Deformations of Logical Clifford Gates

Having reviewed how chain maps can be used to de-scribe logical CZ gates, our work introduces the idea that these logical CZ gates can be locally deformed by certain logically trivial Clifford stabilizers —analogous to the way that logical Pauli gates are deformable by Pauli stabiliz-ers. This will motivate a chain complex description of Clifford operators. As an explicit example, consider the following local, logically trivial, CZ circuit between the two toric codes: 

(6) The circuit acts on only five qubits: a single orange qubit (marked red) is coupled by four physical CZ gates to the four blue qubits surrounding one plaquette, and this lo-cal pattern can be translated anywhere on the lattice. It preserves the code space since it maps stabilizers to sta-bilizers [right of Eq. (6)], and is logically trivial because it changes Pauli operators only by stabilizers—in direct analogy to the Pauli stabilizers in the toric code. Thus, it can be multiplied with a logically non-trivial Clifford gate to obtain a gate with the same logical action, but with a slightly different action on the physical qubits. A general understanding of these gates can be obtained by noting that it maps an X operator to a Z stabilizer (associated with a plaquette). As a result, it can be en-coded in a map Q0 : C0 → C1. Reversing the role of the orange and blue codes gives a distinct Clifford sta-bilizer that maps an X operator to a Z stabilizer, en-coded in a map QT 

> 1

: C0 → C1. More generally, Clif-ford stabilizers are generated from “left diagonal” maps 

Qi : Ci → C−i+1 , as shown on the left of: 

(7) 7In the above equation, the left diagram is not required to be commutative (indicated by the dashed arrows); Q0

and Q1 can be an arbitrary linear maps. As an example, in Eq. (6), Q0 is the map that is zero on all edges ex-cept the one marked in red, which it maps to a plaquette stabilizer, and Q1 = 0. Note that the Qi do not them-selves directly specify a physical gate: gate patterns are instead encoded by “downward” maps C0 → C0 which map qubits to qubits [cf. Eq. 4]. The construction in Eq. (7) converts an arbitrary collection of left-diagonal maps Qi into precisely such a downward chain map [right diagram in Eq. (7)] by composing with boundary and coboundary maps. For instance, its degree zero compo-nent, ∂ ◦ Q0 + Q1 ◦ d, arises from the two paths from 

C0 → C0 in the left diagram of Eq. (7). The result-ing chain maps are called null homotopic [34, 68]; they have trivial action on (co)homology and, through Eq. (4), therefore define logically trivial Clifford gates. For bi-nary chain complexes, all chain maps with trivial action on (co)homology are null homotopic [34]. Section III B 1 provides a detailed treatment of these maps and their associated Clifford stabilizers. Notice that the construction in Eq. (7) is reminiscent of a boundary operation: arbitrary maps Qi are mapped to Clifford stabilizers, similar to how arbitrary collections of plaquettes are mapped to Pauli stabilizers in an ordinary chain complex. We now make this intuition precise.  

> 3. The Chain Map Complex: Unifying Cliffords and Paulis

Having discovered the null homotopic CZ stabilizers of Eqs. (6) and (7), we are naturally led to organizing the 

CZ operators of a code into a chain complex, in analogy to the chain complex description of Pauli operators in Eq. (1). In particular, we show that the CZ gates of a qLDPC code are organized into a chain complex [ C[2] ], which we call the chain map complex (developed formally in Sec. III B 2). The chain map complex comprises a se-quence of vector spaces that correspond to the spaces of maps between two other (co)chain complexes [34, 69], which we label C• and D•:

(8) Mathematically, [ C[2] ]1[C[2] ]0, and [ C[2] ]−1 denote collec-tions of left diagonal (+1 over to the left), downwards (0 over to the left), and right diagonal maps ( −1 over to the left) between two chain complexes respectively. Notably, each of these maps do not need to be chosen such that any of the above diagrams commute. As a remark, the “[2]” label in the chain map complex is used to indicate that it encodes operators at the second level of the Clif-ford hierarchy. We will later discuss how to build chain complexes [ C[ℓ]] describing higher levels. The physical interpretation of the levels of the chain map complex mirrors the chain complex of Eq. (1). Namely, [ C[2] ]1 (maps that run left diagonal) encodes the space of Clifford stabilizers of in Eqs. (6) and (7), mirroring the Pauli Z stabilizers of C1. Moreover, [ C[2] ]0

encodes the full space of CZ circuits (not necessarily log-ical CZ gates) between the two codes, in the same way that C0 in the normal chain complex encodes the space of Pauli Z operators on a code. Finally, the third space [C[2] ]−1 encodes the way in which local CZ gates vio-late the code space. In particular, in contrast to Pauli operators, which generate product states of syndrome vi-olations, Clifford operators generate entangled states of syndrome violations. As a concrete example, we consider the following vector in [ C[2] ]0, which is specified by a collection of maps {fj }:

> D1D0D−1
> d0d1

0 otherwise (9) Such a vector is a basis vector of [ C[2] ]0 and specifies a single CZ gate between the qubit associated with e0 in the orange code and the qubit labeled by e1 in the blue code. It can be checked that the above diagram does not commute, reflective of the fact that a single CZ gate between two codes is not a logical operator. The vector spaces [C[2] ]j above are connected by boundary maps ∂[2]  

> j

; for example, ∂[2] 1 converts left di-agonal maps into downward maps in the same way as was done in Eq. (7). Crucially, elements in the kernel of the boundary map ∂[2] 0 are precisely maps that satisfy the chain map condition, Eq. (5), or equivalently maps for which the diagrams above commute. This tells us that logical CZ gates correspond to the 0-cycles of the chain map complex, ker (∂[2] 0 ). Clifford stabilizers, mean-while, correspond to 0-boundaries in im (∂[2] 1 ), which are automatically 0-cycles since ∂[2] 0 ∂[2] 1 = 0. Moreover, two logical Clifford operators are equivalent if they can be deformed into one another by products of Clifford stabi-lizers. Consequently, different logical Clifford gates are labeled by the homology groups H[2] 0 ≃ ker (∂[2] 0 )/im (∂[2] 1 )! This tells us that the logical Clifford gates of the code are described by the homology of a particular chain com-plex. Since this chain complex can also be used to define a CSS code, this says that logical Cliffords are in corre-spondences with the logical Paulis of an auxiliary code. 8 

> 4. The Chain Map Complex as a Tensor Product Complex

While this insight is tantalizing, at present, it may seem as though reasoning about the code associated with the chain map complex would be challenging. However, as we show in more detail in Section III B 3, the chain map complex for C• and D• is isomorphic to the tensor product D• ⊗ C• [34, 69]: [C[2] ] = · · · [C[2] ]1

> ∂[2] 1

−−→ [C[2] ]0

> ∂[2] 0

−−→ [C[2] ]−1 · · · ≃ D•⊗C•

(10) Intuitively, this follows from the fact that, while chains and cochains in a chain complex are vectors, chain maps are matrices. However, matrices are simply vectors in a tensor product vector space, motivating the fact that the chain map complex is isomorphic to a tensor product complex. The tensor product of chain complexes is closely re-lated to the hypergraph product of the codes C• and 

D• [6, 35] and there is a large body of literature that has built up an understanding of the properties of the tensor product code given its input codes. As a concrete exam-ple, for the 2D toric code, the tensor product complex is simply the 4D toric code with qubits placed on the centers of plaquettes. A physical Z operator on a pla-quette of the 4D toric code is represented via the tensor product of two edge chains f0 = e0 ⊗ e1, which encodes a CZ gate between qubit e0 in one 2D toric code and qubit e1 in the second 2D toric code [c.f. Eq. (9)]. Con-sequently, logical CZ gates of the 2D toric code (cycles in the chain map complex) can be simply “read-off” from the Pauli Z logicals of the 4D toric code! This point is illustrated in Fig. 3 and will be discussed in more detail shortly [Subsection II B].  

> 5. The Chain Map Hierarchy

With the insight that Pauli X and Z gates are described with chain complexes and that CZ gates— traditionally represented as maps between chain com-plexes C• and D•—are also described by a chain complex [C[2] ] ≃ D• ⊗ C•, we are immediately led to a description of higher levels of the Clifford hierarchy within the same language. The insight will follow from the fact that gates at the ℓ-th level of the hierarchy send Pauli’s to gates at the ℓ − 1th level. As an example, note that a CCZ (which lives at ℓ = 3) acting between three codes B•, C •, and D• acts on Pauli 

X by taking it to X × CZ (where CZ lives at ℓ = 2). Hence, intuitively, a CCZ can be viewed as a chain map between the chain complex of code B• into the chain map complex of CZ operators, which is isomorphic to D• ⊗C•.However, as we learned from studying CZ gates, chain maps between two chain complexes themselves form a chain complex. In particular, this chain complex, de-noted [ C[3] ] will be isomorphic to ( D• ⊗ C•) ⊗ B•.                                

> FIG. 3. Logical Clifford Gates Correspond to Pauli Logical Gates in the Tensor Product. Logical Clifford gates between two codes—e.g. two 2D toric code’s TC and
> TC —can be “read-off” from the Pauli logicals of an auxiliary code that is isomorphic to the tensor product of the two codes (in this case, the 4D toric code). (a) Thus, a sheet-like logical
> Zin the 4D toric code (shown in a cross-section at fourth coordinate w= 0) encodes the implementation of (b) an ad-dressable logical CZ gate between two toric codes TC and TC .Explicitly, the 2D sheet is read as a “plot” of the gate. Every edge along yat a fixed xof TC [every orange qubit in (b)] is connected via a CZ gate [red lines in (b)] to every edge along zat a fixed w[every blue qubit in (b)] in TC .The gate pattern is only illustrated for one orange qubit, but the circuit repeats this pattern along y.(c,d) The depth of the corresponding gate is reduced by deforming the logical sheet into a tent shape, so that a qubit at ( x, y ) in TC only has a
> CZ gate with two other qubits in TC .

Iterating the logic above tells us that logical gates of the form Cℓ−1Z (or those related by Hadamards on any qubit) are labeled by homology classes of a chain com-plex [ C[ℓ]] isomorphic to a tensor product of all the codes upon which the gate acts. The collection of chain com-plexes describing gates at each ℓ is dubbed the chain map hierarchy . We develop this construction formally in Sec-tion III C.  

> B. An Application of the Formalism

Thus far, we have discussed how Clifford gates between two CSS codes described by chain complexes C• and D•

are themselves described by a chain complex isomorphic to C• ⊗D•. We now show how this insight can be used to find transversal implementations of (addressable) gates in qLDPC codes (discussed in depth in Section IV). 9

Key Idea 2. Deformations of the logical Pauli operators of the auxiliary code map back to defor-mations of the physical implementation of a logical (non-)Clifford gate, providing a framework for find-ing constant-depth realizations of logical gates at any level of the Clifford hierarchy (when they exist). As a simple illustration of this, we will demonstrate how to easily derive a logical CZ gate between one logical qubit of an orange toric code TC and one logical qubit of a blue toric code TC . This is to be contrasted with the gate of Eqs. (2) and (3), which crucially affects both logical qubits of both toric codes. To construct this gate, recall that, per our formalism, logical Clifford gates are in correspondence with the Pauli logical gates of the code described by the tensor product of two 2D toric codes. This code is simply the 4D toric code, whose logical Pauli gates are well understood. In particular, logical Pauli Z gates of the 4D toric code are given by 2D sheets of Z operators living within the 4D hypercubic lattice. Any logical action of a gate can be encoded in the logical class of a Pauli, and each distinct logical action (e.g. CZ 12 , CZ 21 etc.) corresponds to a distinct Pauli. A canonical representation of one such logical Pauli Z operator in a 3D slice of the 4D lattice is shown in Fig. 3(a). We can view the Pauli logical operator as a plot of the Clifford gate acting between the two toric codes. Specif-ically, let us label the coordinates of TC by x and y and the coordinates of TC by z and w. Then, if the logical 

Z operator of the 4D toric code is supported at coor-dinates ( x, y, z, w ), this means that the qubit living at coordinates ( x, y ) in TC are coupled to the qubits at co-ordinates ( z, w ) in TC via a CZ gate. For instance, for the Pauli logical shown in Fig. 3(a), it translates to the pattern of Clifford gates shown in Fig. 3(b). While the gate in Fig. 3(b) has a depth that scales with code distance, it can easily be checked that the gate enacts an addressable logical CZ gate between a single qubit of TC and TC , as we ultimately desire. If we deform the Pauli logical in the 4D toric code, we are able to reduce the depth of this logical Clifford gate between the two toric codes. In particular, we know that we can, for instance, deform the logical Pauli Z gate in the 4D toric code from a flat sheet to a “tent” [Fig. 3(c)]. As a consequence, the logical is only supported when x > 0. Moreover, for a fixed x > 0 and y there are only two values of z and one value of w such that the logical is supported at ( x, y, z, w ). Consequently, the action of the logical gate between the two toric codes takes the form of Fig. 3(d). We note that the resulting gate still takes a large depth to implement as the same values of z and w

are “hit” for every value of y at fixed x > 0. By further deforming the 4D toric code’s Pauli logical with the same “tenting” trick in the w direction, we find a constant depth implementation of this addressable CZ 

gate. Such an addressable gate is shown in Fig. 4(a).            

> FIG. 4. An Addressable CZ Gate for the Toric Code.
> (a) Further deforming the logical from Fig. 3 results in a transversal logical CZ 1,2gate, acting on only two of the four qubits encoded in two blocks of the toric code. The pattern of gates is shown for a unit cell, and is repeated everywhere in the colored regions of the code. The gate can be made geometrically local by folding the blue toric code. (b) Mov-ing all targets of the physical CZ gates to a single code block results in a transversal logical CZ 1,2gate in one copy of the toric code.

Intuitively, we can understand the action of the gate by folding the TC gate in half and then acting the global 

CZ gate of Eq. (2) between half of the TC code and both layers of the folded TC . With this in mind, we can check that the gates above yields an addressable CZ gate. In particular, the action of this gate on the Pauli logical operators are given by: 

(11) Notice that half of the “horizontal” X logical in TC gets mapped to a full non-trivial Z logical operator in TC .Moreover, the “vertical” X logical in TC maps to a trivial 

Z logical operator. This can be seen by the fact that if the vertical X logical is on the left hand side of TC , it is doubled in the bottom, whereas if it is “cleaned” to the right side, it does not get acted on by the gate. We conclude by noting that by lifting the action of this addressable inter-block gate such that the control and target are in the same code, we get an intra-block CZ 

gate between the two logical qubits of a single toric code. A depiction of the resulting gate is shown in Fig. 4(b). 10  

> III. The Chain Map Hierarchy

Equipped with a conceptual overview of the chain map hierarchy, this section is devoted to formally defining it and developing the technical machinery necessary to practically wield it. The tools developed in this section will be applied in Section IV to discover new logical gates in well-known qLDPC codes. We start by reviewing how CSS codes are described via chain complexes, and how logical Clifford operations— including unitary gates and code surgery measurement gadgets—are instead described as maps between chain complexes (Section III A). While the content of Sec-tion III A is well-known, the section introduces key no-tation and definitions, and is presented in a manner that naturally leads to our main results, emphasizing specifi-cally how abstract homological data is concretely trans-lated into logical quantum operations. By holistically considering the entire space of maps between chain complexes, rather than individual exam-ples, one realizes that maps themselves can be assem-bled into a chain complex—which we call the chain map complex . With this insight, we show how to understand logical Clifford operations on codes in the same chain complex framework used for the codes themselves (Sec-tion III B). Thus, we uncover a notion of deformation for logical gates which is precisely analogous to deformation of Pauli logical gates. This facilitates finding transversal implementations of given logical actions, when possible. Further, by iteratively forming chain map complexes, this framework can be vastly extended to capture logi-cal non-Clifford operations from any level of the Clifford hierarchy. We call the resulting hierarchy of auxiliary chain complexes the chain map hiearchy (Section III C). Even commuting gates within a single code block can be captured with this formalism, facilitating optimal usage of qLDPC codes with many encoded qubits in each code block (Section III D).  

> A. Review: Chain Complexes and CSS Codes, Chain Maps and Logical Clifford Operations

Throughout this paper, we will take the approach of presenting a relevant mathematical structure, and then concretely translating this structure into quantum data or operations. As the simplest illustration of this, we start with the well-known connection between chain com-plexes and CSS error correcting codes [13, 31–33, 70]. In particular, we start by defining a based chain complex as a sequence of vector spaces equipped with privileged bases that will be identified with the qubits, elementary checks, and syndromes of the CSS code. Since we are ultimately interested in families of codes of increasing size, we implicitly consider families of chain complexes with finite bases whose sizes grow along the family. Thus asymptotic terms such as “ O(1)”, “sparse”, and “low-density” are understood with respect to increas-ing code size. We suppress the family index throughout. 

Definition III.1. (Sparse, Based) Chain Com-plexes. A chain complex C• over the binary field 

F2 = {0, 1} is a sequence of F2-vector spaces con-nected by linear maps: 

C• ≡ · · · ∂2

−−→ · · · (12) where ∂j ◦ ∂j+1 = 0 for all j. Elements x ∈ Cj of the degree-j component are called j-chains, the ∂j maps are called boundary maps, and elements of ker( ∂j )are called j-cycles. Associated with a chain complex is a dual cochain complex: 

C• ≡ · · · d−1

−−→ C−1 d0

−→ C0 d1

−→ C1 d2

−→ · · · . (13) Elements x ∈ Cj are called j-cochains and are linear maps x : Cj → F2, i.e. Cj ≃ Hom (Cj , F2), where 

Hom (V, W ) is the space of linear maps from vector space V to W . (If Cj is finite dimensional, Cj ≃ Cj .) The coboundary maps d j : Cj → Cj+1 are defined to satisfy x ◦ ∂j+1 = d j+1 ◦ x for all x ∈ Cj and hence d j+1 ◦ dj = 0 for all j. Elements of ker(d j+1 )are called j-cocycles. The chain complex is based if each Cj has a priv-ileged finite basis B(Cj ) and sparse if, in this basis, the boundary maps ∂j are sparse as matrices—i.e. have O(1) entries per row and column. If the chain complex is based, the cochain complex has a natural basis B(Cj ) from Kronecker pairing: b(b′) = δb,b ′ ,for b, b ′ ∈ B(Cj ), and b ∈ B(Cj ) is dual to b. In this basis, we have d j = ∂T 

> j

as matrices, so that C• is sparse if and only if C• is sparse. When obvious from context, we omit indices from the boundary and coboundary maps, simply writing ∂ and d, respectively. As a matter of notation, we always denote cochains in boldface and chains without boldface, and frequently suppress “ ◦” for composition, simply writing dx, ∂2, etc. Sparse and based chain complexes can be used to spec-ify qLDPC CSS codes, as in the toric code example of Section II and Eq. (1). In the toric code, the basis ele-ments B(C1), B(C0), B(C−1) are associated with the ele-mentary plaquettes, edges and vertices of the 2D square lattice respectively. Theorem III.2. (qLDPC) CSS Codes From (Sparse) Based Chain Complexes. Let C• be a based chain complex over F2, and let the physical qubits be indexed by the basis B(C0). The chosen bases B(C1) and B(C−1) label the elementary Z- and 

X-type stabilizer generators 

Z∂1p p ∈ B(C1),Xd0v v ∈ B(C−1). (16) These generators commute and the subspace of states stabilized by these operators specifies a CSS code. If C• is sparse, the resulting CSS code is LDPC: the stabilizer generators in Eq. (16) have finite weight, and each physical qubit participates in only 

O(1) such generators. 

Proof. The proof of the theorem is standard. From Eq. (15), the commutation between Z∂1p and Xd0v is set by (d 0v)( ∂1p) = v(∂0∂1p) = 0, where the last equal-ity follows from ∂0∂1 = 0. The LDPC condition follows from the sparseness of the (co)boundary maps. The chain-complex description of the CSS code also identifies the syndrome spaces, which record violations of the code space. Concretely, the syndrome of a Zb op-erator is its boundary ∂0b ∈ C−1: its commutation with an X-type stabilizer Xd0v is determined using Eq. (15) by (d 0v)( b) = v(∂0b). Dually, the syndrome of an Xa

operator is its coboundary d 1a ∈ C1, since its com-mutation with a Z-type stabilizer Z∂1p is determined by a(∂1p) = (d 1a)( p). Thus, as depicted in Eq. (1), 

C1, C 0, C −1 encode Z-checks, Z-operators, and viola-tions of X-checks, while C−1, C 0, C 1 encode X-checks, 

X-operators, and violations of Z-checks. The logical Pauli X and Z gates of this CSS code cor-respond to the homology and cohomology classes of the chain complex: 

Corollary III.3. Logical Pauli Gates from (Co)Homology. Given a 0-cycle b ∈ ker (∂0) and 0-cocycle a ∈ ker (d 1), the operators Zb and Xa pre-serve the code space. If a and b live in non-trivial cohomology and homology classes of H0 and H0 re-spectively, defined by: 

Hj = ker (∂j )

Im (∂j+1 ) , Hj = ker (d j+1 )

Im (d j ) , (17) then Zb and Xa act non-trivially on the code space i.e. they are logical Pauli Z and X gates. Thus the number of encoded qubits is k =dim H0(C) = dim H0(C).

Proof. The proof is once again standard. Since the an-ticommutation of Zb with Xd0v is set by v(∂0b), any 

Zb such that b ∈ ker (∂0), will commute with all X-stabilizers and hence preserve the code space. If these 

Zb operators are not stabilizers—i.e. b /∈ Im (∂1)— then they correspond to non-trivial logical Z operators. Hence, logical Z gates are labeled by non-trivial classes in ker (∂0)/Im (∂1) and a similar line of reasoning works for logical X gates.  

> 1. Review of Chain Maps and Logical Clifford Gates

The chain complex formalism provides a clean way of working with the algebra of Pauli operators on CSS codes and, more importantly, provides a way to homolog-ically compute physical implementations of logical Pauli gates. Logical Clifford gates are traditionally described somewhat differently—using maps between chain com-plexes [17]. In particular, as we described in Section II, these gates are treated using chain maps [34, 68]. Keep-ing with the theme of defining mathematical structure and translating it into quantum operations, we now re-view chain maps, and then describe how to associate these with physical implementations of logical Clifford gates. Formally: 

Definition III.4. (Sparse) Chain Map. Given two chain complexes C• and D•, a chain map φ•12 is a collection of linear maps φj : Cj → D−j , i.e. 

φj ∈ Hom( Cj , D −j ), such that the following diagram commutes:

(18) which reverses the arrows, interchanges chains and cochains, and replaces each boundary map with its dual, ∂ ↔ d.If C• and D• are based, we say that φ• is sparse if each of the component maps φj above is sparse as a matrix in the associated bases —i.e. has O(1) entries per row and column. As a matrix, each φT 

> j

is just the transpose of φj and hence is sparse if φ is sparse. This definition generalizes to maps between any pair of chain or cochain complexes: for C• → D•,

C• → D•, and C• → D•, the component maps φj

are respectively from Cj → Dj , Cj → Dj and Cj →

D−j , with the corresponding diagrams required to commute and transposes obtained by dualizing the diagrams. Commutativity of the diagram in Eq. (18) is concretely the condition that 

φj+1 ◦ dj+1 = ∂−j ◦ φj for all j. (20) Once again, when obvious from context, we will omit in-dices from the maps φj , simply writing φ or φ•. In an expression such as ∂ ◦φ or φ◦d, the maps are understood to be the unique ones whose domains and codomains make the composition well-defined. With this conven-tion, the commutativity condition of Eq. (20) is simply 

φ ◦ d = ∂ ◦ φ. 

The commutativity condition ensures that a chain map induces a well-defined map on (co)homology. In particu-lar, a chain map φ• : C• → D• induces maps (φ∗)i : Hi(C) → H−i(D),

[c] 7 → [φi(c)] . (21) Here [ c] = c + Im (d i) denotes the cohomology class of an 

i-cocycle representative c ∈ ker(d i+1 ). The map is well defined because the chain map condition sends cocycles to cycles and coboundaries to boundaries: if c is a cocy-cle, d c = 0, then ∂φ (c) = φ(d c) = 0 , so φ(c) is a cycle. If b = d a is a coboundary, then φ(b) = φ(d a) = ∂φ (a),

so φ(b) is a boundary. Thus ( φ∗)i gives a consistent map from Hi(C) to H−i(D). In the gate constructions below, this induced map is the logical object of interest and the rank of ( φ∗)0 counts the number of independent logicals on which the gate acts nontrivially. Analogous statements hold for the other chain maps used below; for example, a chain map ψ• : C• → D• induces maps (ψ∗)i : Hi(C) → Hi(D). As discussed in Section II, induced maps on (co)homology are precisely what is needed to define logi-cal Clifford gates because logical Pauli Z and X operators are labeled by homology and cohomology classes respec-tively. For a logical CZ gate coupling codes C and D, the Heisenberg action XC 7 → XC ZD gives a nontrivial map from cohomology classes of C to homology classes of D,which can be induced by a chain map φ• : C• → D•. For a CNOT gate, the action XC 7 → XC XD instead gives a nontrivial map from cohomology classes of C to cohomol-ogy classes of D, which can be induced by a chain map 

ψ• : C• → D•. The corresponding transformations of the Z-operators are determined by the transpose maps. Thus, given such chain maps φ• and ψ• , we will consider the following product of physical Clifford gates:

2. Review of Chain Maps and Logical Pauli Measurement 

We conclude our review by noting that chain maps are also traditionally used to describe other Clifford op-erations on codes beyond logical unitary Clifford gates. Specifically, they can also describe the measurement of logical Pauli gates via code surgery [14–16]—which in the Pauli-based framework for quantum computation acts as a logical Clifford operation. Logical Pauli measurement via code surgery is a spe-cific type of code switching protocol [16, 18, 19, 46, 50]. In particular, to measure the logical Pauli operator of a CSS code, one brings in an ancilla system of qubits and uses measurement to “switch” the CSS code into an-other stabilizer code where the Pauli logical operators to be measured are now in the updated stabilizer group. By switching back to the original CSS code, one has then measured the desired logical Pauli operator. In literature, this protocol is typically presented by us-ing a chain map to construct the new system-ancilla sta-bilizers that are to be measured, implementing the code switching [16, 18, 19, 46, 50, 53]. Our presentation follows a slightly different (but ultimately equivalent) approach which facilitates a comparison to the unitary implemen-tation of the Clifford gate [c.f. Fig. 1] [65, 71–74]. In particular, we show that code surgery can be un-derstood as a two step process. First, one performs a transversal Clifford gate between a certain ancilla stabi-lizer state (a hypergraph cluster state) and a CSS code of interest. Second, logical measurement is performed by projectively measuring all of the qubits in the an-cilla state. Crucially, the transversal Clifford gate used is identical in form to the logical Clifford gate of Eq. (22). To establish this formally and make the connection with the unitary gates manifest, we introduce the ancilla stabilizer state of interest: 

Definition III.6. Hypergraph Cluster State. 

The hypergraph cluster state associated to a sparse, based chain complex C• is a CSS stabilizer state on a bipartite system of qubits labeled by elements of the bases B(C−1) and B(C0). The elementary stabilizers of this state are: 

XvXd0v, ZeZ∂0e (31) where v ∈ B(C−1) and e ∈ B(C0). 14 We now show that logical Pauli measurement (via code surgery) follows from a product of Clifford gates between the B(C−1) qubits of the hypergraph state and the qubits of the CSS code of interest [associated with B(D0)], fol-lowed by a complete measurement of the hypergraph state. To make contact with the chain map formalism, it is useful to define the notion of a shifted chain com-plex since gates will now be performed between qubits in B(C−1) and B(D0), which are shifted relative to one another in their respective chain complexes. 

Definition III.7. Shifted Chain Complex.

> B. The Chain Map Complex, Clifford Stabilizers and Clifford Logic

In the previous subsection, we reviewed how a CSS code is encoded by a chain complex: physical Pauli op-erators are chains, Pauli stabilizers are boundaries, and logical Pauli gates are labeled by the resulting homol-ogy classes. On the other hand, logical Clifford opera-tions, including unitary Clifford gates and logical Pauli measurements, are conventionally described rather dif-ferently: as chain maps between the chain complexes as-sociated with CSS codes. We now place these two descriptions on the same foot-ing. We use a mathematical ingredient that is standard in homological algebra: collections of maps between two chain complexes themselves form a chain complex, the internal-hom complex, which we refer to as the chain map complex [34, 69]. Our contribution is to give this mathematical object a physical interpretation as a com-plex of operations on quantum codes. In this dictionary, physical Clifford gate patterns are chains and certain log-ically trivial circuits—which we call Clifford stabilizers —are boundaries. The homology of the chain map complex then labels logical Clifford operations, in direct analogy with the way the homology of the original code complex labels logical Pauli operations. Since the chain map com-plex is itself a chain complex, it defines an auxiliary CSS code. Thus, the chain map complex turns logical Clif-ford operations on the original codes into ordinary Pauli logicals of this auxiliary CSS code. We develop this correspondence in stages. In Sec. III B 1, we first identify Clifford stabilizers, which are local, logically trivial deformations of Clifford gates, analogous to deforming Pauli logicals by Pauli stabiliz-ers. We then introduce the full chain map complex and show that its homology labels logical Clifford operations in Sec. III B 2. Next, we identify the chain map complex with a tensor-product complex in Sec. III B 3. This makes the auxiliary code concrete and turns the search for useful physical Clifford implementations into the familiar prob-lem of finding and deforming Pauli logical representatives within a homology class, which will be exploited later in Sec. IV for constructing explicit logical gates. Impor-tantly, because the chain map complex is itself a chain complex describing an auxiliary code, the construction can be iterated. This recursion produces the chain map hierarchy of Sec. III C, extending the homological chain complex framework to non-Clifford operations arbitrarily high in the Clifford hierarchy. For most of the discussion, we focus on inter-block CZ -type gates between two or more CSS codes. Generaliza-tions to other Clifford gates and to intra-block gates are discussed in Sec. III B 4 and Sec. III D, respectively.  

> 1. Clifford Stabilizers and Null Homotopic Chain Maps

The starting point is the observation, discussed infor-mally in Section II, that logical Clifford gates admit local, logically trivial deformations analogous to the familiar deformations of logical Paulis. We will show that these deformations are generated by Clifford stabilizer circuits that preserve the code space and act trivially on the log-ical qubits. To define Clifford stabilizers formally, we first intro-duce the concept of a null-homotopic chain map [34, 68] [cf. Eq. (7) for the informal sketch], and then discuss how to associate this map with logically trivial Clifford circuits. 

Definition III.10. (Local) Null-Homotopic Chain Maps. A chain map θ• : C• → D• with components θj : Cj → D−j is null-homotopic if there exists a collection of maps Q•, with compo-nents Qj : Cj → D1−j , such that for all integers 

j,

θj = ∂1−j ◦ Qj + Qj+1 ◦ dj+1 . (39) Diagrammatically, the maps Q• appearing in Eq. (39) have the form:                   

> · · · C−1C0C1· · · · · · D1D0D−1· · ·
> d−1d0d1d2
> ∂2∂1∂0∂−1
> Q−1Q0Q1Q2

(40) 16 The dashed horizontal arrows in Eq. (40) indicate that the diagram need not commute. Two chain maps φ• and ψ• are chain homotopic if there is a null-homotopic map θ• such that 

φ• = ψ• + θ•.

This is an equivalence relation. If C• and D• are based, we say that θ• is local (in the LDPC sense) if each of the component maps θj ,written as a matrix in the associated bases, has O(1) nonzero entries in total. This is to be contrasted with a sparse map, which may have O(1) nonzero entries in each row and column. Although written for maps C• → D•, analogous definitions apply for null homotopies between the other chain maps considered above, such as C• →

D•.The distinction between Q• and θ• is important. The maps Qj in Eq. (40) do not themselves specify physical 

CZ circuits. For example, Q0 maps C0 to D1, whereas a physical CZ -gate pattern is specified by a map from C0

to the physical qubit space D0, as in Eq. (22). Instead, Eq. (39) converts Q• into chain maps θj : Cj → D−j

of the form in Eq. (18), whose degree-0 component θ0

specifies a physical CZ -gate pattern via Eq. (22). The two terms in Eq. (39) are the two natural ways to use 

Q to get from Cj to D−j : first apply Qj and then a boundary in D•, or first apply a coboundary in C• and then Qj+1 .

(41) Specializing to the physical degree-zero component which determines gates, θ0 : C0 −→ D0, gives 

θ0 = ∂1 ◦ Q0 + Q1 ◦ d1, (42) A few additional remarks are in order. First, let us explicitly verify that the null homotopic chain maps θ•

defined in Eq. (39) do indeed form chain maps, i.e. they obey the commutation condition in Eq. (20): 

θj ◦ dj = ∂1−j ◦ Qj ◦ dj = ∂1−j ◦ θj−1 (43) where we used d j+1 ◦ dj = 0 in the first equality, and 

∂1−j ◦ ∂2−j = 0 in the second. Second, null homotopic chain maps all have trivial ac-tion on (co)homology. To see this, consider the action of a null homotopic map θ = ∂ ◦ Q + Q ◦ d on a cocycle 

c ∈ ker (d). Note that θ(c) = ∂Q (c) + 0 is a boundary, and thus in the zero homology class. More generally, null-homotopic maps send every (co)homology class to the trivial (co)homology class. Conversely, chain homo-topies are precisely the equivalence relations that identify chain maps with the same homological action [34]: 

Lemma III.11. Trivial Chain Maps as Null Homotopies. Two chain maps φ• and ψ• are chain homotopic if and only if they induce the same action on all (co)homology groups. As a consequence, any chain map with trivial action on homology is a null homotopy. Thus null homotopies are exactly the deformations that do not change the induced logical action of a chain map. (We note that, for this lemma to hold, it is impor-tant that we work over F2 or another field.) Third and finally, to use null homotopies as physical Clifford stabilizer circuits, we require these deformations to be generated locally, mirroring the locality of Pauli stabilizer generators in an LDPC code. The term “local in the LDPC sense” is meant to emphasize the analog of locality appropriate to non-local qLDPC codes: the map θj has support on only O(1) basis elements in total ,although those basis elements need not be nearby in any geometric sense. When no confusion is likely, we simply call such maps local. For sparse based complexes, this locality condition follows directly: 

Lemma III.12. Local Basis for Null-Homo-topic Chain Maps. Let C• and D• be sparse, based chain complexes. Then the vector space of null-homotopic chain maps θ• : C• → D• has a basis of local null-homotopic chain maps. 

Proof. For each j, the space of maps Qj ∈

Hom (Cj , D 1−j ) has an elementary matrix basis deter-mined by the bases of Cj and D1−j . We denote these ba-sis maps by Qc,d j , indexed by c ∈ B(Cj ) and d ∈ B(D1−j ), where Qc,d j (c) = d and zero on all other basis elements. For example, for e ∈ B(C0) and p ∈ B(D1), one basis map is Qe,p  

> 0

: C0 → D1, defined by Qe,p  

> 0

(e′) = δe,e′ p for 

e′ ∈ B(C0) [cf. Eq. (6)]. Since θ• in Eq. (39) depends linearly on Q, applying Eq. (39) to these basis maps spans the space of null-homotopic chain maps. Since C• and D• are sparse, composing a basis map of Q with the (co)boundary maps in Eq. (39) produces only O(1) nonzero matrix entries. Hence each resulting null-homotopic chain map is local. 17 Choosing a linearly independent subset of these spanning maps gives a local basis of null homotopic maps. The preceding discussion has a striking consequence. We are now ready to define Clifford stabilizers, the promised analog of Pauli stabilizers, by associating a physical CZ circuit with the chain map θ• according to Eq. (22): 

Definition III.13. (Local) Clifford Stabilizers. 

Let θ• : C• → D• be a null-homotopic chain map. The physical Clifford circuit

is called a Clifford stabilizer . This is obtained from Eq. (22) with φ = θ.Because θ• is a chain map, U θ

preserves the code space and has a well-defined action on (co)homology. Because θ• is null-homotopic, this induced action is trivial. If θ• is local, then U θ 

> CZ

is called a local Clifford stabilizer . By Lemma III.12, for sparse based chain complexes, the null-homotopic chain maps admit a local basis; the corresponding Clifford stabilizers are called local Clifford stabilizer generators :

CZ CD eC ,∂ 1pD eC ∈ B(C0), pD ∈ B(D1),

CZ CD ∂1pC ,e D pC ∈ B(C1), eD ∈ B(D0). (44) The two generators in Eq. (44) arise from the two con-tributions to θ0 = ∂1Q0 + Q1d1 in Eq. (42). An elemen-tary Q0 maps a qubit label eC to a Z-stabilizer label pD ,while an elementary Q1 maps a Z-stabilizer label pC to a qubit label eD . In the toric code, these are the edge-to-plaquette and plaquette-to-edge constructions discussed in Section II, respectively. Clifford stabilizers thus deform logical Clifford gates in exactly the same sense that Pauli stabilizers deform logical Pauli operators. Let φ• : C• → D• be a chain map defining a logical CZ circuit U φ 

> CZ

via Eq. (22). As shown later in Theorem III.15, every logical CZ action admits at least one physical representative of the form 

U φ

> CZ

. Then, if θ• is null-homotopic, it can be used to construct a different physical representative which is log-ically equivalent: 

U φ 

> CZ

∼ U φ+θ 

> CZ

. (45) Indeed, φ• and φ• + θ• induce the same maps on (co)homology and hence the same logical action. Thus Clifford stabilizers generate the deformation freedom within a fixed homology class of the chain map complex. This allows one to search within that class for simpler physical representatives of a given logical action, for ex-ample representatives of lower depth or sparser support, although such representatives need not always exist. We return to this problem in Sec. IV.  

> 2. The Chain Map Complex and Clifford Logic

The preceding subsection identified the Clifford analog of Pauli stabilizer deformations. In the Pauli case, sta-bilizers are not isolated objects; rather, they appear as 

boundaries in the chain complex that also contains phys-ical Pauli operators and their syndrome violations, with logical Pauli operators labeled by the resulting homol-ogy classes. We now show that Clifford gates admit an analogous chain complex structure. For the chain complex which organizes Clifford gates, the relevant chains are collections of maps between the constituent codes. For clarity, we begin with the inter-block CZ case, involving maps from C• → D•. We have already encountered several such maps. Logical 

CZ gates are specified by a chain map φ•, namely a collection of maps φi : Ci → D−i, depicted as down-ward arrows in Eq. (18), which satisfy the commutativ-ity condition Eq. (20). Clifford stabilizers are specified by null-homotopic chain maps, obtained from a collec-tion of “shifted” maps Q• of Eq. (40), with components 

Qi : Ci → D1−i, shifted diagonally one step to the left relative to the downward maps. Similarly, the code-surgery maps in Eq. (32) have components Ci → D−1−i,shifted by −1, i.e. one step to the right. This section assembles these various Clifford opera-tions and constructions into a single homological struc-ture, in which Clifford stabilizers appear as boundaries and logical Clifford gates are labeled by homology classes. To do so, it is natural to consider collections of maps shifted by an integer j, with components Ci → Dj−i.In fact, these j-shifted collections of maps assemble into a chain complex (See Eq. (8) for a depiction). This is the standard internal-hom complex of homological alge-bra [34, 69], which we denote as [ C•, D •]. We call it the 

chain map complex , reflecting the fact that its cycles are precisely (shifted) chain maps. 

Definition III.14. Chain Map Com-plex/Internal Hom. The chain map complex or 

internal hom associated to a pair of chain complexes 

C• and D• is a chain complex [ C•, D •]:

By Lemma III.11, these classes are exactly the same as classes of chain maps with the same induced action on (co)homology. The chain map complex thus inherits all of the re-lations between chain maps and logical Clifford gates. Given a generic f• ∈ [C[2] ]0, the component f0 specifies a pattern of CZ gates by the same rule as Eq. (22):

In particu-lar, the logical measurement gadgets associated to chain maps φ• : C[−1] • → D• (Theorem III.8) are associated to ( −1)-cycles of [ C[2] ]−1, and homologous cycles encode gadgets that measure the same pattern of logicals. This relation between the chain map complex and Clif-ford logic is summarized by the following theorem. 

Theorem III.15. Clifford Logic and the Ho-mology of the Chain Map Complex. Consider the chain map complex [ C[2] ]• ≃ [C•, D •]. Then the following are true: 1. Homology and Logical Clifford Gates: Each ho-mology class [ φ] ∈ H0([ C[2] ]•) determines a unitary logical CZ action coupling codes C and 

D. Any representative φ• of the class specifies a unitary logical CZ circuit U φ 

> CZ

of the form Eq. (22). Moreover, if two logical CZ circuits of this form act differently on logical qubits, they are labeled by different homology classes of H0([ C[2] ]•). 2. Homology and Logical Pauli Measurement: 

Each homology class [ φ] ∈ H−1([ C[2] ]•) deter-mines a logical Z-Pauli measurement pattern on code D. Any representative φ• specifies a physical code-surgery gadget M φZ of the form Eq. (33). Moreover, if two code surgery gad-gets of this form measure different patterns of logical qubits, they are labeled by different ho-mology classes of H−1([ C[2] ]•). 3. Exhaustiveness: For any logical CZ action, there is logical CZ gate of the form U φ

> CZ

19 [Eq. (22)] labeled by a homology class of 

H0([ C[2] ]•) that performs that action. More-over, for any Pauli Z measurement on logical qubits, there is code surgery gadget of the form 

M φZ [Eq. (33)] labeled by a homology class of 

H−1([ C[2] ]•) that performs that action. The first two statements follow directly from Lemma III.11 together with Theorems III.5 and III.8. Exhaustiveness follows from the fact that any linear ac-tion on (co)homology is achieved as the induced action of some chain map, with a direct proof being given in Appendix A 2. A few remarks are in order. First, while the above theorem is phrased in terms of logical CZ gates and logical Pauli Z measurements, it can be generalized to other classes of gates and X measurements easily as will be discussed shortly in Section III B 4. Second, the correspondence between homology classes and logical actions is generally not one-to-one. As dis-cussed above, a homology class specifies the induced maps Hi(C) → H−i(D) for all degrees i, whereas the logical CZ action depends only on the degree-zero map 

H0(C) → H0(D), where the qubits live. Thus, dis-tinct homology classes can differ in their action at other degrees while inducing the same action on the logical qubits. We make this redundancy explicit below using the tensor-product description of the chain map complex. Third, the exhaustiveness clause of the above theo-rem states that for any pattern of CZ gates on logical 

qubits, there is a pattern of CZ gates of the form of Eq. (22) that one can perform on physical qubits to im-plement this action. Crucially, this does not imply that this pattern of gates can necessarily be implemented in constant depth i.e. it does not impose that φ is sparse (cf. Theorem III.5). However, since the pattern of gates on physical qubits are associated with homology classes of [ C[2] ]•, they can be deformed with Clifford stabiliz-ers as in Eq. (45) to lower their depth. In some cases, this leads to transversal implementations of these Clif-ford gates and we give examples of this in Section IV. However, we leave the general problem of determining when these high depth gates realize constant depth im-plementations to forthcoming work [75]. The chain map complex has the same structural in-gredients as the chain complex of an ordinary CSS code: chains, boundaries, and homology classes, each with a direct physical interpretation. This suggests promoting the analogy one step further and viewing the chain map complex itself as the chain complex of an auxiliary code. 

Corollary III.16. Chain Map Complex as a qLDPC CSS Code. Let C• and D• be sparse, based chain complexes. Then the chain map com-plex [ C[2] ] ≡ [C•, D •] is itself a sparse, based chain complex and hence defines an auxiliary qLDPC CSS code by Theorem III.2. 

Proof. The corollary follows immediately from the pre-ceding results. The chosen bases of C• and D• induce the elementary-map basis of [ C[2] ]• (analogous to the discus-sion below Lemma III.12), and Eq. (48) shows that its boundary maps are sparse whenever those of C• and D•

are sparse. The auxiliary-code interpretation then fol-lows from Theorems III.2 and III.15. Then, to summarize and synthesize the discussion of this section, the chain map complex is a new auxiliary chain complex encoding operations on codes C and D:physical CZ -gate patterns are chains in the chain map complex, Clifford stabilizers are boundaries, and logical 

CZ gates and logical Pauli measurements are labeled by the resulting homology classes. The auxiliary chain map complex can itself be viewed as a CSS LDPC code. Then, logical Pauli operators of this auxiliary code encode log-ical Clifford operations on codes C and D. 

> 3. Chain Map Complex as a Tensor Product

By identifying logical Clifford gates with homology classes of the chain map complex, the preceding sub-section places them in direct analogy with Pauli logical operators in an auxiliary CSS code. To make this per-spective useful, however, we need a more physical picture of the auxiliary code. As presently formulated, the chain map complex is a rather abstract object about which we have little intuition: its chains are collections of (shifted) maps between two chain complexes, which are much less familiar than the chains of the original CSS chain com-plex. The goal of this subsection is to give an equally concrete picture of the chain map complex, in order to be able to think about it more intuitively and concretely. We will do this by identifying the chain map complex with a familiar tensor-product complex. It is helpful to make an analogy to vector spaces. Chain complexes resemble vector spaces in many ways—not surprising, as they are made from a sequence of vector spaces. In this sense, we should think of chain maps as matrices relating vectors. The statement that chain maps can be organized into a chain complex is the analog of the fact that matrices themselves form a vector space. In fact, the concrete realization of the space of linear maps between finite dimensional vector spaces V → W as a vector space is well known: it is isomorphic to W ⊗ V ∗,where V ∗ is the dual vector space of linear functionals on 

V .

Hom (V, W ) ≃ W ⊗ V ∗. (53) Under this isomorphism, a map which takes |v⟩ → | w⟩

is sent to a product |w⟩ ⟨ v|. The isomorphism is then 20 simply the statement that linear maps are spanned by products of kets in W and bras in V ∗.Returning to chain complexes, the space of linear maps 

C• → D• is the chain map complex we have already discussed. The tensor product of chain complexes is also a familiar concept which has appeared extensively in the quantum error correction literature. It is closely related to the hypergraph product of CSS codes. 

Definition III.17. Tensor Product of Chain Complex. Given chain complexes C• and D•, the tensor product C• ⊗D• is a chain complex with com-ponents (C• ⊗ D•)j = M

> i∈Z

C−i ⊗ Di+j , (54) and boundary map defined by its action on pure ten-sors 

∂⊗(c ⊗ d) = ( ∂c ) ⊗ d + c ⊗ (∂d ). (55) For tensor products of cochain complexes C• or D•,one replaces any C−i or Di+j with Ci or D−i−j , respec-tively. This is to maintain the ordering of cochain com-plexes written in Eq. (13), where coboundary maps go from left to right (see Appendix B for more details). For example, (C• ⊗ D•)j = M

> i∈Z

Ci ⊗ Di+j . (56) The isomorphism (53) motivates the following theo-rem, which expresses that maps between chain complexes can be reshaped into chains in a tensor product. 

Theorem III.18. Chain Map Complex as a Tensor Product. For based chain complexes C•

and D•, there is an isomorphism between the chain map complex [ C•, D •] and the tensor product of D•

and the dual complex of C•:[C•, D •] ≃ D• ⊗ C•. (57) Equation (57) is the chain complex analog of Eq. (53): the space of maps between chain complexes is isomorphic to the tensor product of the target complex D• and the dual of the control complex C•. Dualizing swaps chains and cochains, so that if C• appears in the chain map complex, C• appears in the tensor product. Unlike vector spaces, there is usually no isomorphism between a chain complex and its dual, C•̸ ≃ C•, so it is important to keep track of dualizations in Eq. (57). For instance, a map 

ψ : C• → D• encoding a CNOT is a chain in D• ⊗ C•,which can be a very different space to D• ⊗ C•, which encodes CZ gates. Indeed, whether or not transversal gates can be found in these complexes may depend on the dualizations: later, in higher levels of the hierarchy, we will find CCZ gates in a complex like C• ⊗ C• ⊗ C•,but one expects to never find logical CCNOT gates [76] in C• ⊗ C• ⊗ C•.Theorem III.18 gives a concrete handle on the auxil-iary CSS code defined by the chain map complex. Rather than working with an abstract complex of maps, we can view it as a tensor-product CSS code and use the geom-etry and deformability of its Pauli logicals to guide the construction of Clifford gates on the original codes C, D .The proof of Theorem III.18 is again standard in ho-mological algebra [77]. We recall it (in greater generality than needed here—including for non-binary chain com-plexes relevant for non-qubit codes) in Appendix B. The tensor-product description makes two features of Theorem III.15 particularly transparent: the exhaustive-ness of the logical CZ construction, and the fact that distinct homology classes can nevertheless have the same logical action. By the K¨ unneth formula [34],

C and D, the logical CZ action depends only on the 

i = 0 sector, H0(D) ⊗ H0(C), which is isomorphic to Hom (H0(C), H 0(D)) . Thus, an element of H0(D) ⊗

H0(C) specifies precisely the induced map on the logical 

X operators of code C.In particular, if C and D encode kC and kD logi-cal qubits, respectively, this sector has dimension kC kD .Choosing bases {LCβ }kC 

> β=1

> α=1

of H0(C) and

Arbi-trary elements of this kC kD -dimensional sector therefore realize arbitrary patterns of the kC kD possible pairwise logical CZ gates, giving a direct explanation of the ex-haustiveness statement in Theorem III.15. At the same time, the remaining i̸ = 0 sectors in Eq. (58) do not affect the action on the encoded qubits. Consequently, distinct homology classes that differ only in these other sectors can implement the same logical CZ action. Thus 

H0([ C•, D •]) organizes the full homological action of chain maps, while its H0(D) ⊗ H0(C) sector captures their action on the encoded qubits. As a matter of notation and to make the tensorial per-spective manifest, it will sometimes be helpful to keep track of indices when manipulating chains, cochains, and chain maps (especially, when discussing non-Clifford and intra-block gates). A consistent assignment of indices would be to associate chains with vectors with upper in-dices bα, and cochains with vectors with lower indices 21 

aα.5 Accordingly, a map f : C• → D• is represented by a tensor f αβ , while a map g : C• → D• is represented by the tensor g βα . Their actions map cochains to chains and cochains respectively:
