C. The Chain Map Hierarchy and Non-Clifford Logic 

Having identified Clifford operations with the homol-ogy classes of an auxiliary chain complex, the generaliza-tion to non-Clifford operations—arbitrarily high up the Clifford hierarchy—is straightforward. The construction is recursive and, at this point, almost automatic! 

1. Chain Map Hierarchy 

The chain map hierarchy organizes operations arbi-trarily high up the Clifford hierarchy into chain com-plexes. At a conceptual level, the construction mirrors the recursive structure of the Clifford hierarchy [66]. We index the hierarchy so that Pauli operators form the first level. Clifford gates occupy the second level, and map 22 Paulis to Paulis; third-level gates map Paulis to Cliffords; and, more generally, gates at each higher level map Paulis to operators from the level immediately below. 

Climbing the chain map hierarchy means recur-sively iterating the chain map complex construction of Sec. III B 2 to ascend the Clifford hierarchy. At a given level ℓ of the Clifford hierarchy, operations from that level are packaged into a chain (map) complex, which can be associated with an auxiliary code; Pauli logicals of that auxiliary code encode logical operators at that level. Maps into that auxiliary code then define a new chain map complex, whose Pauli logicals encode logical operations at the next level, ℓ + 1. This recursion is already visible in the passage from 

CZ to CCZ . At the Clifford level, a logical CZ maps a logical X in the control code C• into a logical Z of code D• The logical Z is an ordinary Pauli logical of an ordinary code, and logical CZ ’s are encoded by maps 

C• → D•. After the Clifford construction, however, the logical CZ gate has itself been repackaged as a cycle in an auxiliary chain map complex [ C[2] ] = [ C•, D •]. This means that, at the next level of the Clifford hierarchy, CZ 

can play exactly the same role that Z played one level below! Correspondingly, [ C[2] ]•, which is a chain complex in its own right, can play the same role as D• to iterate the chain map complex construction (Definition III.14) to build an auxiliary code at the next level. For example, a logical CCZ maps a logical X in a new control code B•

into a logical CZ on codes C• and D•, so it is encoded by maps B• → [C[2] ]•, or equivalently by the iterated chain map complex [ C[3] ] ≡ [B•, [C[2] ]•] = [ B•, [C•, D •]]. To define the chain map hierarchy formally, we fix a collection A of chain complexes describing codes of inter-est. We mathematically define the chain map hierarchy before showing how it encodes physical and logical oper-ations at arbitrary levels of the Clifford hierarchy. 

Definition III.19. Chain Map Hierarchy. Let

Since the tuple does not specify whether an entry is used as a chain complex or a cochain complex, we suppress bullets and dualizations inside C[ℓ], restoring them only when writing the chain map complex [ C[ℓ]] associated with a particular operation. By construction, each [ C[ℓ]]•

is itself a chain complex: every new level is obtained by applying the chain map complex construction of Defi-nition III.14 to a chain complex from the previous level, with boundary maps inherited recursively. Thus, at fixed level ℓ, the hierarchy contains many distinct chain map complexes, indexed by the participating code blocks and the operation type. Specializing for now to Cℓ−1Z-type operations, it is use-ful to see how the construction works in the first few lev-els. 2. Tensor Product Structure of the Chain Map Hierarchy 

Having defined the chain map hierarchy, we will find it useful to extend Sec. III B 3 to provide a concrete tensor product interpretation to the iterated chain map complexes populating the hierarchy. We proceed induc-tively. At the Clifford level, Theorem III.18 identifies the ordinary chain map complex with the tensor product chain complex: [C•

> (i2)

, C (i1)•] ≃ C(i1)• ⊗ C(i2)•. At the next level, we apply the same theorem again, now with the previous auxiliary complex playing the role of the target: [C•

> (i3)

, [C•

> (i2)

, C (i1)•]] ≃ [C•

> (i2)

, C (i1)•] ⊗ C(i3)• ≃

C(i1)• ⊗ C(i2)• ⊗ C(i3)•. Iterating this inductive structure arrives at the following general result: 

Theorem III.20. Iterated Chain Map Com-plex as a qLDPC Tensor Product Code. Let 

C[ℓ] =  C(i1), . . . , C (iℓ)

 be an ordered length-ℓ tu-ple of sparse, based chain complexes, with ℓ fixed. Then an iterated chain map complex [ C[ℓ]] ∈ C [ℓ] 

> A

,which belongs to the ℓth level of the chain map hi-erarchy, is itself a sparse, based chain complex and hence defines an auxiliary qLDPC CSS code by The-orem III.2. Moreover, it is isomorphic to a repeated tensor product of the constituent chain complexes: [C[ℓ]] ≃

> ℓ

O

> j=1

C(ij ). (67) In Eq. (67), chain and cochain bullets are suppressed; their orientations are inherited from the chosen iterated chain map complex [ C[ℓ]]•. The theorem follows induc-tively from Theorem III.18 and Corollary III.16. Thus each iteration of the chain map construction ap-pends one new tensor factor. Continuing the analogy of reshaping matrices into vectors, chains in an ℓ-th-level complex may equivalently be viewed as ℓ-leg tensors. Be-cause the constituent chain complexes are based, their tensor product carries the corresponding product basis. More generally, once a chain in an iterated chain map complex is identified with an ℓ-leg tensor, the same object may be depicted either in chain-map form or in tensor-product form. Thus a degree-zero chain f ∈ [C[ℓ]]0 may be written as a tensor f α1α2··· αℓ , where αj labels the leg associated with C(ij ). Viewed as a multilinear map, f acts on ℓ − 1cochains to produce a chain, generalizing Eq. (59): 

bα1 = f α1α2··· αℓ aα2 · · · aαℓ . (68) Other contractions with combinations of cochains aαj , or even other tensors gαj αk ··· , produce chain or chain-tensor outputs from other combinations of C(il) inputs. This tensor-product viewpoint suggests a graphical cal-culus for the hierarchy. Chains in ordinary chain com-plexes are one-leg tensors, chains in chain maps are two-leg tensors, and chains in higher level iterated chain map complex encoding higher-level operations are higher-leg tensors. In this sense, the chain map hierarchy is a tensor-network-like language whose legs are chain com-plexes rather than ordinary vector spaces. The graphical calculus of these tensor networks could generalize the tra-ditional quantum circuit formalism to naturally encode the separation between operations on physical qubits and logical qubits. 

3. Chains of [C[ℓ]]• and Physical Non-Clifford Gates 

We now explain the unitary-gate dictionary at level ℓ

of the chain map hierarchy. The dictionary directly gen-eralizes the Clifford case: for a given chain map complex [C[ℓ]], 0-chains specify physical gate patterns, 0-cycles specify code-space-preserving logical gates at the ℓth level of the Clifford hierarchy, and 0-boundaries specify gen-eralized stabilizer circuits with trivial logical action. Ac-cordingly, gates corresponding to cycles in the same ho-mology class of H0([ C[ℓ]]) have the same logical action. In this subsection, we develop the first part of this dic-tionary by associating 0-chains with physical gates; the interpretation of cycles, boundaries, and homology will be developed in the subsequent subsections. For nota-tional simplicity, we spell out the construction for the 

Cℓ−1Z-type branch of the hierarchy. Other gate types are obtained by changing the chain/cochain orientations, as in Sec. III B 4. Let f• ∈ [C[ℓ]]0 = [ C•

> (iℓ)

> (iℓ)

In the tensor-network in-terpretation, this gives an ℓ leg tensor with one leg for each component code. The physical qubits of each code 

C(i) live in degree 0 so, as before, to associate f with physical operators, we first restrict to the degree-zero 24 component f0 : C0(iℓ) → [C[ℓ−1] ]0. For ℓ > 2, how-ever, the target is itself a collection of maps, [ C[ℓ−1] ]0 =L

Definition III.21. Sparse Tensor. An ℓ-leg ten-sor T α1α2··· αℓ is sparse if, after fixing any one index 

αj , only O(1) choices of the remaining indices yield a nonzero component T α1α2··· αℓ .For ℓ = 2, this is the usual finite row- and column-weight condition which defines a sparse matrix. It is useful to quantitatively relate the sparsity of f to the circuit depth of U f

> Cℓ−1Z

> Cℓ−1Z

> Cℓ−1Z

ℓ(∆( f ) − 1) other gates, so the gates can be partitioned into ℓ(∆( f )−1)+1 sets of mutually disjoint gates. Thus, if f is sparse, then ∆( f ) = O(1) and U f 

> Cℓ−1Z

4. Chain Maps, Homology, and Logical Non-Clifford Unitary Gates 

We have now understood how 0-chains of [ C[ℓ]] specify physical Cℓ−1Z-gate patterns. We next consider the role of 0-cycles. As in the Clifford case, the 0-cycle condition promotes a physical gate pattern to a chain map: the resulting gate preserves the joint code space, descends to a well-defined action on homology, and hence defines a logical operation. We now develop this correspondence. The role of 0-boundaries as generalized stabilizers and deformations of these logical gates is discussed in the next subsection. Once again, we focus our discussion on Cℓ−1Z type gates, but generalizations to Cℓ−1NOT or to any gates related to Hadamard transformations on any of the af-fected qubits is straightforward, and proceeds by replac-ing cochain complexes C•  

> (ij)

with chain complexes C(ij )

> •

on the relevant codes, as discussed for the Clifford case in III B 4. We first establish the key algebraic property of a 0-cycle φ ∈ ker( ∂[ℓ]0 ) ⊆ [C[ℓ]]0: under its action, 0-cocycles of any constituent code are mapped to 0-cycles one level lower in the hierarchy, while 0-coboundaries are mapped to 0-boundaries. This directly follows from iterating the chain-map property from the Clifford case. In particular, it implies that changing the input cocycle by a cobound-ary changes its image only by a boundary, so that φ in-duces a well-defined map from the cohomology of a con-stituent code to the homology of the level-( ℓ−1) auxiliary complex. To make this precise, fix a constituent code C(ij ) and use the reshaped presentation of Eq. (76). In this pre-sentation, φ is a chain map from C•

As in the Clifford case, the full homology H0([ C[ℓ]]) generally contains additional sectors with some kj̸ = 0. These sectors do not affect the action on the encoded qubits, which is determined entirely by the all-degree-zero logical tensor. Consequently, distinct homology classes may implement the same logical gate. Theorem III.22 guarantees that a 0-cycle φ defines a well-defined logical gate, but it does not guarantee that the corresponding physical gate is transversal: this addi-tionally requires a sparse representative of the homology class. In general, such representatives need not exist. For example, the Bravyi–K¨ onig theorem forbids transversal realizations of gates arbitrarily high in the Clifford hier-archy for Euclidean topological codes [67], and no error-detecting stabilizer code admits a transversal CCNOT 

(Toffoli) gate between three identical code blocks [76]. Thus, a central practical question is whether a given log-ical gate admits a sparse representative within its homol-ogy class. The freedom to search for such representatives comes from adding 0-boundaries, which leave the logi-cal action unchanged while deforming the physical gate pattern. We turn to these higher-level stabilizer defor-mations next.  

> 5. Non-Clifford Stabilizers and Boundary Deformations

We now turn to the 0-boundaries of the chain map hi-erarchy. These play the same role for non-Clifford gates at higher levels of the Clifford hierarchy that Pauli stabi-lizers and Clifford stabilizers played at the first two levels: they specify physical gate patterns with trivial logical ac-tion, and hence generate deformations of a logical gate within a fixed homology class. In analogy to Clifford stabilizers, we consider null-homotopic chain maps θ, i.e. 0-boundaries of the level-ℓ

chain map complex:

Thus, just as Pauli and Clifford stabilizers allow one to locally deform physical representatives without changing their logical action, non-Clifford stabilizer circuits of the form Eq. (III C 5) allow us to search within a fixed ho-mology class for sparser representatives of a non-Clifford logical gate. This is the deformation freedom relevant for finding lower-depth, and when possible transversal, implementations.

D. Intra-block Gates in the Chain Map Hierarchy 

Thus far, our discussion has primarily centered around logical gates that are performed between code blocks. We 29 conclude this section by discussing how the chain map hierarchy can further be used to describe a large class of logical gates that act within a code block. When generalizing to the intra-block case, new chal-lenges naturally arise. At a high level, note that our goal will be to associate chain maps φ• at some level of the chain map hierarchy to gates U φ acting on physical qubits. Our previous associations between chain maps and (non-)Clifford gates focused on multiply-controlled Z

or X gates—e.g. a chain map φ• : C• → D• described a pattern of inter-block CZ gates between qubits e and tar-get φ(e). However, in the intra-block case, “control” and “target” qubits can be the same qubit. Consequently, we need to revisit how we associate chain maps to physical gates. 

1. Commuting Intra-block Clifford Gates 

We begin our discussion of intra-block gates by first considering the case where the gates in a block all commute—specifically, are diagonal in some basis—and are Clifford, before returning to non-Clifford and non-commuting generalizations at the end. Naively, to specify an intra-block gate on code C, we would simply need to think about chain maps φ• : C• →

C•. We will see now that this is, in general, not sufficient. In particular, in this case, we will need to supplement our chain map description of gates with two extra ingredients. First, we will need to work with a subcomplex of sym-metric chain maps (i.e. chain maps φ that are equal to their transpose φT). 6 Second, these chain maps will act on a Z4 “lift” of the chain complex describing the code we are interested in. The motivation for these two ingredients is straight-forward. In particular, chain maps φ• : C• → C• should intuitively be associated to intra-block gates that map X

operators to Z operators under the group commutator. There are two saliant Clifford gates that do this: the CZ 

gate and the S = ei π  

> 2n

gate, which act on X operators as: [CZ e,e ′ , X e]grp = Ze′ [Se, X e]grp = −iZ e (95) where e̸ = e′. The association of chain maps to CZ gates is more familiar from our discussion on inter-block gates. There, an element φeC ,e ′ 

> D

= 1 implied that XCe maps under the gate to ZDe′ —there is a CZ between qubits at eC

and e′ 

> D

. However, if all gates connect between the same code block, then a CZ e,e ′ gate will map both XCe 7 → ZCe′       

> 6Strictly speaking, we do not need that a chain map is symmetric on the level of chains but only require it to be induce a symmet-ric matrix on (co)homology. If this is the case, φand φTwill be homotopic and our formulas for gates will pick up a correc-tion arising from the null homotopy connecting them. This is directly analogous to the role that the higher cup product plays in defining the Pontryagin square.

and XCe′ 7 → ZCe . For φ to encode the correct action on 

X operators, it must thus be symmetric, φe,e ′

= φe′,e .That is, φ = φT.The chain map should also encode S gates, correspond-ing to an X that maps to a Z supported on the same site. However, note that the S gate does not square to 1, as is the case with the CZ gate. Instead, S4 = 1. Conse-quently, a normal binary chain map φ mapping between two chain complexes over F2 would not be able to dis-tinguish between say S and S†. This motivates a need to “lift” our chain complexes to be defined over Z4. We now formally introduce these ingredients and show how they can be used to define intra-block logical Clifford gates. We start by incorporating the symmetry constraint into our chain complex description. To do so, let us first recognize that imposing that φαβ

> j

(Cj−1) specifies the pre-image of Cj−1 un-der ∂j .Checking that the above forms a chain complex is straightforward. For the purpose of diagonal Clifford gates, we will be interested in a constrained subcomplex of the chain 30 map complex subject to the constraint S as defined in Eq. (96), [ C•, C •]S. As a remark, we note that this par-ticular subcomplex has a a nice interpretation from the tensor product viewpoint. In particular, [ C•, C •]S is iso-morphic to the subcomplex of symmetric tensors —i.e. tensors where φαβ = φβα . Consequently, it is more de-scriptive to call the constrained subcomplex [ C•, C •]S the 

symmetric subcomplex of [ C•, C •]. The symmetric subcomplex handles the symmetry con-straint alluded to earlier. All that is left is to introduce the concept of a Z4-lift of the chain complex, which will allow us to handle the Z4 nature of the S gate. A Z2n-lift of a chain complex is defined as follows: 

Definition III.25. (Nice, Sparse) Chain Com-plex Lift. Let C• be a based chain complex asso-ciated with a sequence of vector spaces Cj over the field F2 and boundary maps ∂j . We say that a chain complex eC• over the ring Z2n is a Z2n-lift of C• if: (1) The spaces eCj 7 associated with eC• are related to the vector spaces of C• via Cj = eCj /2 eCj .(2) As matrices, the boundary maps of eCj satisfy 

∂j = e∂j mod 2. In other words, there exists a quotient chain map π• :

eC• → C• that is surjective and ker (πj ) = 2 eCj . We further require that there is a quotient on cochains ˆπ• : eC• → C•, with the same properties and ˜ a(˜b) = ˆπ(˜ a)( π(˜b)) for all ˜ a ∈ eC•, ˜b ∈ eC•.We say that eC• is nice if its induced maps on (co)homology are surjective—i.e. every (co)cycle in 

C• is the image of a (co)cycle in eC•. Moreover, we say that eC• is a sparse lift of C• if eC• is a sparse based chain complex. A lift of a chain complex provides us a formal way of extending a binary chain complex to one defined over, say, Z4. While one might imagine that a lift is as sim-ple as mapping the elements 0 , 1 ∈ F2 to the elements 0, 1 ∈ Z4, this “naive lift” doesn’t ensure that the char-acteristic properties of chain complexes hold over Z4, e.g. 

e∂j ◦ e∂j+1 may not equal to zero modulo 4 if we use the naive lift. Further, even if a lift can be found, it is not guaranteed to be either sparse or nice: the matrices ˜∂j

may be densely filled with even elements, and some cycles in C• need not be quotients of cycles in eC•. Nevertheless, it was proven in Ref. [78] that a nice lift of a binary chain      

> 7Formally, the spaces eCjare Z2n-modules—analogs of vector spaces but where the underlying field is replaced by a ring (see Appendix B)

complex to Z2n (for arbitrary n) always exists—though such a lift is still not guaranteed to be sparse. Sparse lifts are guaranteed to exist for large classes of qLDPC codes, include all hypergraph product codes of classical codes, certain codes achieved through other product construc-tions (e.g. certain balanced product and fiber-bundle product codes), and even good qLDPC codes [78]. These are not guaranteed to be nice but nice and sparse lifts are conjectured to exist for some of these in Ref. [78]. Equipped with a Z4 lift of a chain complex C•, eC•, and a symmetric subcomplex [ eC•, eC•]S, we can associate its 0-chains and 0-cycles with intra-block Clifford gates. In particular, the way of doing so is given a 0-cycle eφ• of [ eC•, eC•]S, we can define the eφ0 as the matrix associated with the map eφ0 and use this to define the following unitary gate:

Let us note that because CZ 2 = 1, only the data of eφ modulo two is relevant for these gates. In contrast, the full Z4 data of eφ is necessary for defining the S gate piece. However, since S gates are single qubit gates and only the CZ gates contribute to the depth of the gate, the depth of V eφ is only determined by the sparsity of eφ modulo two! Note that eφ modulo two defines a chain map φ• ∈ [C•, C •]S. Consequently, a viable strategy for finding constant depth implementations of intra-block di-agonal Clifford gates is simply to find a sparse chain map in [ C•, C •]S and then use the non-sparse but nice lift of Ref. [78] to “correct” the diagonal contribution eφe,e to an appropriate logical intra-block gate. 3. A Few Remarks on Non-Clifford and Non-Commutative Intra-Block Gates 

We conclude with some brief remarks on non-Clifford and non-commutative intra-block logical gates (i.e. intra-block gates where individual gates don’t mutually com-mute). While we leave a full treatment of these gates 32 to forthcoming work, we describe some simple ways to define these gates that we will utilize in Section IV. We saw for intra-block CZ gates that only the Z2 chain map φ• : C• → C• was needed to find a logical gate, and not the more general Z4 lift. This simplification extends to higher level of the chain map hierarchy, and indeed, we can also avoid the need to “correct” the diagonal part of the gate. For any 0-cycle φ• ∈ [C[ℓ]]0 = [ C•, [· · · , C •]] 0,we define a logical Cℓ−1Z gate. In fact, the naive appli-cation of Eq. (62) defines this gate, 

V φℓ = exp ( iπφ α··· γ nα · · · nγ ) . (113) However, a few remarks are in order. In general, Eq. (113) is a circuit both of Cℓ−1Z gates and also Cℓ−2Zs, 

Cℓ−3Zs, all the way down to Zs. Whenever some of the indices in the sum over nα · · · nγ coincide, the number of 

n operators in the sum reduces ( nene = ne) resulting in a gate from lower in the Clifford hierarchy. Similarly, V φℓ ’s logical action includes both Cℓ−1Z pieces and corrections from lower in the hierarchy. Further, note that we do not need φ• to be symmetric. Nonetheless, our previous remarks about the action of any gate on operators Xa still holds, and one can see that 

V φℓ has such a symmetric action, regardless of asymmetry in φ•. In fact, the logical action of V φ

Crucially, un-like CℓZ gates, different CNOT gates do not commute with one another and hence a complete theory of these gates would need to account for this non-commutativity by tracking the order of the gates. as a matrix. In other words, there is a set of controls

> IV. Discovering Transversal Gates Made Simple

The chain map hierarchy provides a structured method to search for transversal gates in general qLDPC codes. In this section, we derive several examples of previously-unknown gates uncovered by the chain map hierarchy. Most of the codes we consider, including the usual two-and three-dimensional toric code, have been studied ex-tensively in the literature, but nonetheless have transver-sal gates which had not been found. While logical gates can be systematically enumerated with the chain map hierarchy, the gates so obtained are 33 not automatically transversal. For the codes we con-sider here, the isomorphism between the chain map com-plex and tensor product gives an intuitive geometric way to sparsify chain maps, when possible, and thus obtain transversal gates. (The general conditions under which a candidate chain map can be sparsified will be explored in upcoming work [75].) While the gates in this section are all novel, our empha-sis is primarily on the method of employing tensor prod-uct structure and sparsification to discover gates (out-lined in Sec. IV A) rather than the particular utility un-locked by the new gates. In Sec IV B we derive transversal logical gates for the entire Clifford group in the two-dimensional toric code. In Sec. IV C we move to the next level of the chain map hierarchy, and obtain addressable CCZ gates in the three-dimensional toric code. Further moving beyond mani-fold codes, in Sec. IV D we derive many addressable Clif-ford gates in a fracton code with many encoded qubits— the anisotropic linon-planon (ALP) code. Finally, in Sec. IV E, we extend this non-manifold example to ad-dressable CCZ gates in the tensor product code of ALP and the repetition code. As a remark, our results refute some folklore that ad-dressable CZ gates, say, require the existence of a global transversal CCZ gate, and similar for higher levels of the Clifford hierarchy [22, 63, 80, 81]. The toric code, for instance, has no transversal CCZ , but does have an ex-haustive suite of addressable CZ s.  

> A. Recipe for Discovering Gates

All of the gates we find in this section are constructed with the same basic recipe. Fixing a collection of codes 

C[ℓ] = ( ..., C, D ), the recipe is as follows (Fig. 5). (1) Specify a logical action of the gate as an element (φ∗)0 ∈ H0(D) ⊗ H0(C) ⊗ · · · .(2) Sparsify a representative cycle of the logical action using chain map hierarchy stabilizer deformations. (3) Synthesize the physical implementation of the gate and enjoy computing with your qLDPC code. 

Bon appetit .This recipe reiterates the procedure outlined in Secs. II and III. Specifying any desired logical action from a fi-nite level in the chain map hierarchy, that action can be encoded as a logical tensor ( φ∗)0 (the all-degree-zero component of φ∗). This is the exhaustiveness of Theo-rem III.22. Then, picking any representative cycle φ• of this homology class, we get a chain tensor that can be turned into a gate. However, this tensor will usually be dense, so one needs to sparsify the tensor by deforming with boundaries. With that done, a physical implemen-tation of the gate can be synthesized with Eq. (69). Specify Sparsify Synthesize            

> FIG. 5. Recipe for discovering gates: To find a transver-sal logical gate using the chain map complex, first specify the desired logical action: aCZ 12 between one pair of the four encoded qubits in two blocks of the toric code, say. Then,
> sparsify a representative of this logical action using the chain map complex stabilizer deformations. Finally, synthesize the gate by converting the chain map to a physical quantum cir-cuit.

The key step in the recipe is (2), sparsifying the gate by deforming with stabilizers. In full generality, this is algo-rithmically hard—similar to finding the minimum weight logical of a CSS code. Nonetheless, step (2) may be easy if all the codes in C[ℓ] are well-understood, such that the full space of deformations is simple. In this section, we will focus on the case where each of the codes in C[ℓ]

is a tensor product of well-understood classical codes, 

D• = d(1)  

> •

⊗ d(2)  

> •

⊗ · · · .In this case of tensor product codes, the recipe can be unpacked into more explicit steps. Gates from the 

ℓth level of the chain map hierarchy are constructed as follows. 

(1) Specify: First, choose a target logical action of the gate. (a) Logical action: The logical action is specified as a homology class (φ∗)0 = LD ⊗ LC ⊗ · · · ∈ H0(D) ⊗ H0(C) ⊗ · · · (119) as in Eq. (79) and Theorem III.22. In Eq. (119), we specify an addressable action—( φ∗)0 is rank 1 as a tensor, and affects the minimum possible combina-tion of logicals from each code. More general ( φ∗)0

(4) (Optional) Intra-block gates: To find intra-block gates, one needs to find a chain tensor ˜ φ• with additional properties (Section III D). In this section, we will find transversal intra-block implementations of Cℓ−1Z gates (which can be produced from any inter-block φ• by sym-metrization), S gates (which can be found with a Z4 lift of the chain complex), and CNOT gates (which can be found from inter-block gates with disjoint controls and targets). In the sparsification step (2(b)), it is not guaranteed that every candidate logical action φ∗ will admit some regrouping in Eq. (122) that results in easily sparsified factors. Indeed, if this were always possible, we could find a transversal implementation of a universal gate set for any code by looking high enough in the chain map hierarchy, violating various no-go theorems [67, 76, 82]. In this section, “easy to sparsify” will be a synonym for “line-like logicals,” in which case geometric intuition will allow the identification of sparse representatives. Then, the logical regroupings that work will be those such that each factor has at most one line-like logical ld1 

> j

(say), and every other lc1 

> j′

, and so on are point-like. (Because the factor codes c(i) 

> •

are classical, they have point-like Z

logicals.)  

> B. Full Clifford Group in the 2D Toric Code

In this section, we apply the recipe of Sec. IV A to dis-cover new transversal gates in the toric code [13] which generate the entire Clifford group. It is quite remarkable that, despite almost three decades of study, there are sev-eral transversal gates in the toric code which were previ-ously unknown, such as an addressable Hadamard affect-ing just one of the two encoded qubits. While very recent work [83] used a numerical search to find transversal im-plementations of the Clifford group in small instances of the rotated surface code, it has not been clear until now how to find such gates in the full asymptotic toric code family of increasing code size.  

> 1. New Gates and Exhaustiveness

In the following subsections, we find transversal im-plementations of all of the following addressable gates. Logical action is denoted with a bar, and the physical gates in the constant depth circuit realization of gates are also listed. 

• Phase gates: ¯S, as a circuit of CZ and S(†);

• Controlled Z: CZ , as a circuit of CZ ; and 

• Controlled not: CNOT , as a circuit of CNOT .The single qubit logical gates can act on any of the 2 N

encoded qubits in N ≥ 1 blocks of the toric code, and the two-qubit gates act on any pair, including those within 35 the same code block. Further, we prove that, between the gates above and the known 

• Global Hadamard (known): ¯H ⊗ ¯H within a single code block, as a physical H on every qubit followed by a reflection (swap) around a diagonal [54–56, 84], all possible transversal logical gates in N blocks of the toric code are now exhausted. That is, any logical ac-tion that can be implemented by transversal gates in the toric code can be implemented by an O(1) depth circuit composed of the above logical gates. (The number of blocks N is treated as a constant for scaling statements.) This includes, in particular, the addressable Hadamard mentioned in Sec. I. 

• Addressable Hadamard: ¯H, as a circuit of CZ , S(†),and H.The exhaustiveness of this gate set is a consequence of the observation that the above gates generate the en-tire Clifford group in any number of toric code blocks. The Bravyi-K¨ onig theorem implies that all logical gates formed from geometrically local circuits in the toric code are Cliffords. We conjecture that Bravyi-K¨ onig con-tinues to hold for all finite depth circuits of few-body but possibly non-local gates (we present an informal proof for manifold codes in Section VI), and in the two-dimensional toric code this statement has already been proved in Ref. [76]. 

Lemma IV.1. The transversal logical gates listed above generate the entire Clifford group on any num-ber N of toric code blocks. 

Proof. We compile the ¯H ⊗ ¯H, ¯S, and CNOT gates into a known generating set for the Clifford group: consisting of all ¯H, ¯S, and CNOT gates. Henceforth, we drop bars on logical gates. We are only missing single-qubit Hadamards. Label two qubits from the same block 0 , 1. Then the gate H0

is given by 

H0 = e−iπ/ 4S0(H0H1)S0(H0H1)S0, (126) and similarly for H1. This follows from ( HS )3 = eiπ/ 4

and H2 = 1.

Theorem IV.2. The transversal logical gates above are exhaustive: they generate all possible logical ac-tions realizable by transversal gates in N = O(1) blocks of the two-dimensional toric code. 

Proof. Lemma IV.1 shows that all Clifford gates are pos-sible to achieve in O(1) depth (in code distance) as prod-ucts of the gates we have identified. By [76, Corollary 10, Example 8], no family of constant locality (that is, O(1) sparseness), constant depth circuits on the family of two-dimensional toric codes can implement a non-Clifford log-ical action on sufficiently large distance codes. Thus, the available gates generate all possible logical actions of transversal gates. The exhaustiveness of our generated gates does not exclude a potentially more efficient (lower depth) real-ization of these gates. However, we already have the optimal O(1) scaling of circuit depth with code distance: all (finitely many at fixed N ) elements of the Clifford group are realized as O(1) combinations of finite depth circuits. Our scaling with the number of code blocks N

may not be optimal. Finally, we comment on the relation of our results to a recent no-go theorem in Ref. [85], which states that it is impossible to realize the full Clifford group with “fold transversal” Clifford gates in a stabilizer code with more than one qubit. We use a less restrictive notion of transversality than Ref. [85], which demands that the entire Clifford group be possible to implement with a depth-1 circuit, while we only demand O(1) depth in code distance. As such, the no-go of Ref. [85] is not relevant to our setting. Before proceeding to derive the new gates, we review the chain complex presentation of the toric code, and its classical factor code the Ising model.  

> 2. Chain Complex Formalism for the Ising Model and Toric Code

While the toric code needs little introduction, our cal-culations in this section are couched in the chain com-plex perspective on the toric code and its tensor product structure, and in the particular conventions developed in Section III. In particular, while the toric code is usually presented in terms of a square cellulation of a torus, with chain complex components living in degrees 2 (plaque-ttes), 1 (edges with qubits) and 0 (vertices), we insist that qubits are always at degree 0. As such, the toric code chain complex should have components at degrees 1, 0, and −1, so that qubits are still placed in the mid-dle component. This convention also affects how one in-terprets the toric code as a tensor product of repetition codes—one of the codes should have components at de-grees 1 and 0, while the other should be at 0 and −1. We use this subsection to review the toric code using these conventions. The toric code chain complex, TC •, is the tensor prod-uct of an Ising model (repetition code) complex Ix• and its dual I•

> 3. Interblock Gates

Continuing to label qubits by x and y and two code blocks by orange and blue, the four possible addressable 

CZ gates are: 

CZ xx , CZ xy , CZ yx , CZ yy . (144) Our formalism finds transversal implementations of all of these, and similarly for CNOT . We describe a represen-tative set of these below. 

(1) CZ yx gate: The first application of the recipe is to an addressable logical CZ yx between two code blocks of the toric code, represented by orange and blue colors. One realization of this gate has already been discussed in Sec. II B, and is shown in Fig. 4(a). We find an inequiv-alent implementation of the same logical action, shown in Fig. 6. Following the steps of the recipe in order, we first spec-ify the logical action. > 4. Intra-block Gates

As discussed in Section III D, chain maps can also spec-ify commuting intra-block gates, acting on only one copy of the toric code. However, in our construction, these chain maps must obey some additional constraints, de-pending on whether they describe CZ , ¯S, or CNOT gates. Here, we describe intra-block gates of these kinds, and also the addressable Hadamard from Section IV B 1. 39 

(1) CZ xy gate: Intra-block diagonal Clifford gates ( CZ 

and ¯S) are obtained from sparse, symmetric Z4-valued chain maps eφ• acting on a sparse, nice lift of the chain complex TC •. However, for CZ gates, we can essentially ignore the Z4 lift and instead just directly use the chain map φ• for CZ yx that we already found, via Eq. (113). The resulting logical action becomes symmetrized (up to a ¯Z logical). For the case of our φ•, we get a symmetrized logical action 

Lx 7 → Ly , Ly 7 → Lx. (162) This corresponds to an intra-block CZ xy gate, as ex-pected. The procedure to construct the corresponding physical gate is to move the controls of the physical CZ gates in the CZ yx circuit down to the blue target block, and replace gates by Z operators when controls and targets collide. Such an intra-block logical CZ x,y gate is shown in Fig. 4(b). 

(2) ¯Sx gate: Lifting the CZ xx gate to an ¯Sx requires paying attention to the Z4 lift. However, as we have constructed the chain map corresponding to CZ xx from a tensor product of chain maps on classical codes that have obvious lifts, finding the Z4 version of the chain map reduces to repeating the calculations we already made, but now using the Z4 Ising model and Z4 toric code. Additionally, we must ensure that the chain map we find is symmetric. The Z4 Ising model ˜I• has the same basis of edges ˜ ei ∈

B(˜I1) and vertices ˜ vi ∈ B(˜I0), and a boundary map ∂˜ei =˜vi+1 − ˜vi. The corresponding coboundary map is d˜ vi =˜ei−1 − ˜ei. We have a degree-1 self-duality ˜ κ• : ˜I• ∼

−→ ˜I•,˜κ0(˜ vi) = ˜ ei, ˜κ1(˜ ei) = −˜vi+1 , (163) and tent map ˜ τ• : ˜I• → ˜I• for odd distances d,˜τ1(˜ ei) = ˜ τ1(˜ ed−1−i) = ˜ vi (0 ≤ i ≤ d−12 ), (164a) ˜τ0(˜ vi) = −˜τ0(˜ vd−i) = −˜ei−1 (1 ≤ i ≤ d−12 ), (164b) and even distance ˜τ1(˜ ei) = ˜ τ1(˜ ed−i) = ˜ vi (0 ≤ i ≤ d/ 2) , (164c) ˜τ0(˜ vi) = −˜τ0(˜ vd+1 −i) = −˜ei−1 (1 ≤ i ≤ d/ 2) . (3) CNOT xy gate: A sufficient condition for being able to realize a previously-interblock logical CNOT xy as an intra-block gate is that, when controls and targets are moved to the same block, no control acts on the same qubit as any target. This is a fairly harsh requirement, but we see from inspecting Fig. 6 that it is almost sat-isfied by the CNOT xy gate of the toric code. Or more correctly, this disjointness condition is almost satisfied by a translated chain map: 

φ• = σ ◦ (Td/ 2τ ⊗ κ−1τ Tκ−1), (171) where Tδ : I•∼

−→ I• is a translation by δ. Both controls and targets only act on half the system, so translating by half the distance results in nearly non-overlapping gates. Any intersections at the boundaries can be resolved by not using a complete tent for τ•, but instead a tent with a flat top of O(1) width, as illustrated. 

(172) This achieves the non-overlapping condition, and allows an intra-block CNOT xy gate. The physical circuit for the CNOT xy gate is schemati-cally illustrated as follows (with many gates omitted). 

(173) As in the ¯Sx gate, a vertcal ¯Xx logical triggers a line of 

CNOT gates, producing a perpendicular ¯Xy logical. An ¯Xy logical can be moved into the target region, and is affected trivially. Due to the compression of the support of the circuit through the flat top tent, some qubits con-trol more gates than they did in the interblock CNOT xy 

gate. 

(4) ¯Hx gates: While we have not constructed a con-cise description of Hadamard gates as chain maps, the proof of Lemma IV.1 in Sec. IV B 1 shows how an address-able Hadamard can be constructed from an addressable ¯S and a Hadamard-conjugated ¯S (a gate of the signature (−1) m/ 2). Either of these are handled by the chain map formalism, and so we also have an addressable Hadamard as a product of the synthesized physical circuits. In par-ticular, an addressable ¯Hx gate is realized as follows. 

(174) The outer gates of the product are ¯Sx and the inner gate is ¯Sx conjugated by the global logical Hadamard of the toric code, ¯H ⊗ ¯H. 

> C. Diagonal Non-Cliffords and Hadamard-free Cliffords in the 3D Toric Code

The recipe of Sec. IV A also applies to higher levels of the chain map hierarchy. This section applies the recipe to the three-dimensional toric code [86], finding an ad-dressable CCZ gate, as well as Hadamard-free Cliffords (generated by S, CNOT ). The three dimensional toric code can be expressed as a tensor product of three Ising models, 

3TC • ≃ Ix• ⊗ Iy• ⊗ I• 

> z

. (175) When placing qubits at the degree-0 component, this pat-tern of chain and cochain complexes corresponds to a three-dimensional toric code with one-dimensional Z log-icals. We label logical qubits by {x, y, z }, corresponding to the extended direction of their ¯Z operators, so that ¯Xα logicals span planes orthogonal to α.Note that, unlike the two-dimensional toric code, the three-dimensional toric code is not self-dual, 3TC •̸ ≃

3TC •. As such, the three-dimensional toric code cannot have a transversal Hadamard. Indeed, the Z-distance of 

3TC • is d (the linear dimension of the three-dimensional torus), while the X-distance is d2. Any circuit which conjugates a line-like ¯Z to a plane-like ¯X must, by the pigeonhole principle, act on a single qubit with at least 

d two-body gates.  

> 1. Interblock Gates

(1) CNOT xz gate: The lack of a transversal Hadamard means that we cannot derive an addressable CNOT xz by conjugating a CZ xz . Instead, we have to find the gate directly. Following the recipe, we start with the tensor product representation of the desired logical action,

> x

> z

FIG. 7. Addressible CCZ zxy gate in the 3D toric code. 

Physical CCZ gates act on one xy quadrant in the red code, one y half of the orange code, and the entire blue code. The three qubits on which each CCZ gate acts are (i) a red qubit, (ii) either the orange qubit in the same position as the red, or at a position reflected through the yz plane, and (iii) either a blue qubit at a position shifted by a half-unit-cell translation (in all axes) from the orange qubit, or at a position reflected through the zx plane.

(183) which describes the gate of Fig. 7. To digest the structure of this gate, we examine its action on plane-like ¯X logicals of three stacks of the three-dimensional toric code (Fig. 8). Considering red ¯X logicals first, we observe that only ¯Xz logicals ori-ented in the xy -plane cannot be deformed to completely avoid the gate, so only this qubit is affected by the gate in the red code. Conjugating such an ¯Xz logical by the gate produces a circuit of CZ gates supported in the same 

xy -plane as the ¯Xz logical [Fig. 8(a)]. The controls are supported only on the left hand side of the orange code, but the targets act on the entire plane in the blue code. This is simply the generalization of the addressable CZ 

of the two-dimensional toric code (Fig. 4) to the three-dimensional case. The only orange ¯X logical which can-not avoid the gate is that in the yz -plane, ¯Xx, which intersects the half-plane in a line. The microscopic X

operators along this line are each acted upon by two CZ 

gates, with targets in the blue code related by a reflection 42 of y. The result of conjugating the ¯Xx logical is thus a blue ¯Zy logical running along the y direction, perpendic-ular to the ¯Xy logical in the zx -plane [Fig. 8(b)]. The logical action of the gate is thus 

CCZ zxy , (184) consistent with the guarantee of the recipe. 

(3) No Toffoli gates: It is likely impossible to find a transversal logical CCNOT αβγ gate (Toffoli gate) in the three-dimensional toric code. Indeed, if it were possible to implement any logical CCNOT αβγ , these could be used to compile any CℓNOT gate from higher in the Clifford hierarchy, violating a (still hypothetical, though see Sec-tion VI) extension of Bravyi-K¨ onig to nonlocal circuits. In terms of the recipe, while one can easily find dense logical circuits which implement CCNOT αβγ , there is an obstruction to sparsifying their associated chain ten-sors. Such tensors correspond geometrically to four-dimensional logicals in a nine-dimensional toric code. In order to be transversal, only O(1) gates can affect a fixed qubit e in the red code. This means that the intersec-tion of the four-dimensional cycle with any of the six-dimensional cardinal planes formed by fixing three of the coordinates to correspond to the qubit e must be O(1). This is not possible: the intersection of these four- and six-dimensional surfaces in an ambient nine-dimensional space will generically be one-dimensional, and the num-ber of gates acting on e will grow with the linear di-mension of the codes. This holds even for nonlocal gates. Compare this to the CCZ αβγ case: the logical corresponds to a three-dimensional surface [the product of curves in Eq. (183)], which can be made to only have point-like intersection with any cardinal six-dimensional plane in nine ambient dimensions. Indeed, each of the factors in Eq. (183) has at most two intersections with any car-dinal plane. This argument is given more generally in Section VI.  

> 2. Intra-block gates

(1) CCZ zxy gate: As discussed in Section III D 3, to re-alize the multi-block logical CCZ zxy within a single block, we can just apply Eq. (113). The result is logical and, up to corrections by CZ and ¯Z operators (all of which we know how to perform in 3TC ), acts on ¯Xα operators with the symmetrized logical action of φ• from Eq. (183). We claim this action is simply 

CCZ xyz . (185) Indeed, the unsymmetrized φ∗ is rank-1: it takes a par-ticular logical from each code (red, orange, or blue) to the 

CZ between the other two logicals in the other two codes. The symmetrization takes any logical to the symmetrized 

CZ between the other two logical qubits—precisely the logical action of CCZ xyz .It is instructive to compare this result to what we would get with a non-addressable CCZ gate. There, the symmetrization of the logical action would include an even number of CCZ xyz actions, arising from different combinations of the interblock gates. The resulting gate would thus be trivial logically. 

(2) ¯Sx gate: Single-logical-qubit gates, such as a Clifford ¯Sx gate, cannot be constructed by symmetrizing some non-symmetric φ•. Rather, we must find a symmetric, sparse chain map acting on a sparse, nice Z4 lift. To find an ¯Sx gate in the 3D toric code, we can extend the corresponding lifted chain map for the 2D toric code. Indeed, the Z2-logical tensor corresponding to a CZ xx 

action is

(3) CNOT xz gate: As in the 2D toric code, intrablock 

CNOT gates can be constructed from interblock circuits where control qubits and target qubits are disjoint. The

> D. Addressable Cliffords in a Non-Manifold Fracton Code

The chain map hierarchy formalism, and the general recipe of Sec. IV A, extend beyond manifold codes such as the two- and three-dimensional toric codes. In this section, we demonstrate the construction of address-able Clifford gates in the anisotropic-linon planon (ALP) code: a geometrically three-dimensional code with frac-tonic excitations confined to either planes or lines [87–89]. 43  

> 1. The Anisotropic Linon-Planeon code

The ALP code is also a tensor product of classical codes, namely the plaquette Ising model [87], PI •, and the (dual) Ising model, I•,

ALP • ≃ PI xy • ⊗ I• 

> z

. (190) Subscripts x, y, z again denote axes labels. The code properties of the ALP code descend from its classical factor codes, of which the plaquette Ising code is the less familiar. The plaquette Ising model is described by a two-term chain complex with basis vectors 

B(PI 0) placed on the vertices of a two-dimensional dx ×dy

square lattice, labeled by vij . The B(PI 1) basis vectors are labeled by plaquettes, and the boundary map sends a plaquette to the sum of four vertices bordering it, 

PI • =



 . (191) As an equation, 

∂p ij = vij + v(i+1) j + vi(j+1) + v(i+1)( j+1) , (192) with i ∈ Zdx and j ∈ Zdy regarded with periodic bound-ary conditions. Logicals in the plaquette Ising model—cycles in PI 0—are given by lines of flipped bits running horizontally or vertically. Unlike in the toric code, these lines cannot be deformed: they must remain straight. Similarly, cycles in PI 1 correspond to sums of plaquettes along horizonal or vertical lines. 

(193) As the structural similarity of these cycles suggests, the plaquette Ising model is self-dual with periodic boundary conditions, PI • ≃ PI •[−1]. As a code, the plaquette Ising model has many encoded bits, corresponding to the many horizontal and vertical lines in the plane. However, an important feature of the plaquette Ising model is that the natural line-like collec-tions of cycles in H0(PI ) and H1(PI ) actually do not form a basis. The full collection of lines is overcomplete: flip-ping all horizontal rows of bits is the same as flipping all vertical rows of bits, so the sum of all lines is zero. The total number of encoded bits (the dimension of H0(PI )) is dx + dy − 1, the number of lines less one linear depen-dency. We fix a basis for the (co)homology classes as follows. Let i ∈ Zdx ∪ Zdy be a label for a column ( i ∈ Zdx with fixed x) or row ( i ∈ Zdy with fixed y) of vertices in the lattice. For i̸ = 0 x ∈ Zdx , denote the cohomology class spanned by the row (column) of vertices i by a0

i ∈ Zdy , and a0i = [ p0x0y + pi0y ] for i ∈ Zdx .The rigidity of the line-like logicals is what makes the plaquette Ising code, and its descendant the ALP code, a fracton model. Indeed, consider implementing a partial logical from H0(PI ) by flipping bits along a straight line segment. This produces four violated stabilizers, two at each endpoint of the segment. Such pairs of excitations can be moved by partial logicals, but only along straight lines, and only if those lines are perpendicular to the dis-placement of the excitations. These line-mobile pairs are called linons. Single excitations are immobile, in that they cannot be moved without increasing their energy. 8

In condensed matter language: the line-like symmetries

44 are not deformable 1-form symmetries, but rather sub-system symmetries. The ALP code, resulting from the tensor product Eq. (190), is geometrically three dimensional, with qubits arranged on the vertical edges and horizontal plaquettes (that is, in the xy plane) of a cubic lattice. The Z and 

X stabilizers are 

. (197) ALP logical operators are horizontal (in the x or y direc-tions) or vertical (in the z direction) lines of X or Z,

. (198) As in plaquette Ising, the line-like logicals of ALP are overcomplete. The product of all horizontal Z logicals is trivial, and similarly for X logicals. As such, each of the vertical X and Z logicals anticommutes with two of the horizontal logicals. Similarly, the product of four vertical logicals at the corners of a square is a product of stabilizers. Accounting for all these redundancies—or using the K¨ unneth formula—the total number of encoded logical qubits in the ALP code is 2( Lx + Ly − 1). Truncating a horizontal logical produces two excited stabilizers at each boundary, and these exitations can move in the plane spanned by the logical direction (one of x or y) and the Ising direction 

z. These are called planons. On the other hand, vertical logicals ¯Zzi and ¯Xzi cannot be deformed (much) transver-sally, and truncating them produces single-stabilizer ex-citations which can only move vertically without growing in energy. These are the linons.

(4) Addressable CNOT gates: As in the toric code, the ALP code has a self-duality ALP •∼

−→ ALP •. This allows any CZ gate to be converted to a CNOT by com-posing its encoding chain map with the self-duality. 46 

(5) Intra-block off-diagonal gates: The ALP code has a transversal Hadamard, implemented by aHadamard on every qubit followed by a swap of qubits across a diagonal x = y. Using this, we can compile ad-dressable Hadamard gates using addressable ¯S gates, as in Lemma IV.1 and Eq. (174). For instance, this gives us an addressable ¯Hixy gate. In turn, these Hadamards can be used to construct CNOT gates. Alternatively, we can observe that the targets and con-trols of the CNOT gates frequently do not intersect. This certainly happens when the planes where the gates act are parallel and separated by at least one unit cell. It also occurs when they are orthogonal: in the CNOT analogue of Fig. 9, the orange controls can be translated parallel to their plane without changing the action of the gate. Thus, the controls can be made to avoid the plane of the targets. With disjoint control and target qubits, CNOT 

gates can be realized as intra-block CNOT gates, simply by moving all controls and targets to a single code block.

> V. A Universal Form of the Cup Product from the Chain Map Hierarchy

Thus far, we have shown that the chain map hierar-chy provides a conceptually clear language for both un-derstanding computation with qLDPC codes, and find-ing logical operations therein. We now discuss the re-lationship between the chain map hierarchy and other approaches to understanding logical gates. From Section III, it is manifestly clear how several approaches to Clifford computation naturally fit into the chain map hierarchy language. In particular, ho-momorphic CNOT gadgets [17, 57], homomorphic mea-surement gadgets [14–16, 18, 19, 42–53], automorphism gadgets [90–94], and fold-transversal gates [54–56] on qLDPC codes are regularly phrased in terms of chain maps—which are simply labeled by cycles of chain com-plexes at the second level of the chain map hierarchy. However, known non-Clifford gadgets are not typically understood via chain maps and how they relate to the chain map hierarchy is not as manifest. A popular approach to understanding these gadgets is through the use of cup products —bilinear maps on cochains that induce a product on the cohomology of a given chain complex. It has been shown that if a chain complex is equipped with such a product, it is possible define sparse cohomology invariants which in turn en-ables defining certain non-Clifford gates [20–23]. Con-sequently, a flurry of recent work has been devoted to developing constructions of cup products and their co-homology invariants on chain complexes associated with qLDPC codes. These works take a variety of different approaches. Some generalize the cup product associated with man-ifolds to other chain complexes by either exploiting the sheaf structure naturally associated with particular qLDPC codes, or by mapping a qLDPC code of interest to a high dimensional manifold [22, 23, 25, 26]. Others bypass building a true product structure on cohomology and instead attempt to define the cohomology invariants directly on codes with certain algebraic requirements on their (co)boundary maps [21, 58, 59]. In this section, we show that these approaches can be understood and extended within the chain map hierar-chy. In particular, after reviewing the notion of a cup product and their associated cohomology invariants in Subsection V A, we show how chain maps at the third level of the chain map hierarchy completely parameter-ize cup products on qLDPC codes—a fact that follows from the universal property of the tensor product (Sub-section V B). Moreover, each cohomology invariant de-fined from these specifies an element of the chain map hierarchy. Consequently, our formalism can uncover cup products and associated gates that are not accessible through prior methods.  

> A. Review: Cup Products, Cohomology Invariants, and Logical Gates

We start by defining precisely what we mean by a cup product on chain complexes, before discussing how it can be used to define gates. While much of this subsection is review, we remark that we will present these ideas in a way that will naturally lead into our subsequent results. Broadly speaking, a cup product associated with a chain complex is defined as follows: 9

Definition V.1. (Sparse) Cup Product. Sup-pose that C• is a cochain complex over F2. We say that a function 

⌣: M

> p,q

Cp × Cq → M

> p+q

Cp+q (226) (where Cp ⌣ Cq ⊆ Cp+q ) defines a cup product on 

C• if ⌣ is bilinear and satisfies the Leibniz rule , i.e. d( a ⌣ b) = d a ⌣ b + a ⌣ db (227) where a ∈ Cp and b ∈ Cq and p and q are arbitrary integers. Further suppose that C• is sparse and based. We say that ⌣ is input sparse in degree p and q if for all xp ∈ B(Cp), there are O(1) elements xq ∈ B(Cq )such that xp ⌣ xq̸ = 0 and vice versa. We say that ⌣ is output sparse in degree p and q, if two conditions are met. First, for all xp ∈ B(Cp) and 

xq ∈ B(Cq ), xp ⌣ xq has O(1) support on basis ele-ments in B(Cp+q ). Second, for all xp+q ∈ B(Cp+q ), there are O(1) pairs xp ∈ B(Cp) and xq ∈ B(Cq ) such that xp+q ∈ support( xp ⌣ xq ). Here, support( a) ⊆

B(Cp+q ) is the set of basis elements x for which 

a(x)̸ = 0. Any cup product that is both input and output sparse in all degree pairs is simply called sparse.   

> 9We note that some definitions of the cup product [68] require the product to be associative—as it is in its original setting of simplicial complexes—in which case it induces a ring structure on cohomology. Since we are interested in defining gates from the cup product—for which associativity is not necessary—we omit associativity from the definition.

48 The Leibniz rule of Eq. (227) ensures that the cup product induces a bilinear product on the cohomology classes. This can be seen by observing two facts. First, the cup product of two cocycles is also a cocycle. Second, the cohomology class of the cup product of two cocycles depends only on their cohomology class. We can see the first by noting that if a and b are co-cycles, then a ⌣ b satisfies: d( a ⌣ b) = d a ⌣ b + a ⌣ db = 0 (228) where the first equality follows from the Leibniz rule and the second equality follows from the fact that (1) a and 

b are cocycles and so their coboundary must be zero and (2) bilinearity ensures that 0 ⌣ b = a ⌣ 0 = 0. Hence, the cup product of two cocycles is also a cocycle. We can see the second by noting that if a and b are cocycles and d c is an arbitrary coboundary, then: 

a ⌣ (b + d c) = a ⌣ b + a ⌣ dc (229) = a ⌣ b + ( a ⌣ dc + d a ⌣ c) (230) = a ⌣ b + d( a ⌣ c) (231) and a similar calculation can be done for ( a + d c) ⌣ b.Consequently, a ⌣ (b + d c) and ( a + d c) ⌣ b are related to a ⌣ b by a co-boundary. Therefore, the cohomology class of [ a ⌣ b] depends only on the cohomology classes [a] and [ b] and we can define on induced bilinear product on cohomology by [ a ⌣ b] = [ a] ⌣ [b].  

> 1. Concrete Example: The Ising Model and the Toric Code

To provide a concrete example of the cup product, we introduce the cup product for the 1D Ising model and show how this can be used with the tensor product to define the cup product for the toric code in 2D and higher dimensions. Let us recall that the Ising cochain complex I• is a sparse based chain complex where B(I0) and B(I1) corre-spond to the vertices and edges of a one-dimensional line graph with the global topology of a circle: 

v0

e0

v1

e1

v2

e2

v3

e3

v4

e4eN −1 · · · · · · (232) The cup product on the 1D Ising model may be defined via the following rules on the basis cochains 

vi ⌣ vj = δi,j vi, (233) 

vi ⌣ ej = δi,j ej , ei ⌣ vj = δi,j −1ei (234) where vi ∈ B(I0) and ei ∈ B(I1). Outside of these cases, the cup product is zero. Intuitively, the rules above com-municate that the cup product of two vertices is non-zero if they are the same, the cup product of a vertex with an edge is only non-zero if the edge is directly to the right of it, and the cup product of an edge with a vertex is only non-zero if the edge is directly to the left of the vertex. This effectively picks an orientation on edges. The cup product on general cochains is defined by applying the rules above bilinearly. It is furthermore easy to verify that the cup product above satisfies the Leibniz rule. We can use the cup product for the Ising model to de-fine a cup product for the 2D toric code and its higher di-mensional variants. In particular, recall from Section IV, that the 2D toric code is given by TC • ≃ Ix• ⊗ I• 

> y

. In this section, to make contact with conventions for the cup product, we always use cochain complexes concentrated in non-negative degrees. As such, we will shift the toric code up a degree relative to Section IV, so that it has non-zero cochains in degrees 0 , 1, 2. This shifted toric is isomorphic to a tensor product of two Ising cochain complexes: 

C• = I•

⊗ I• 

> y

≃ TC [1] •. (235) Basis elements of C0 are of the form vi ⊗ vj , the basis elements of C1 are of the form vi ⊗ ej and ei ⊗ vj , and basis elements of C2 are given by ei⊗ej . The cup product on C• is inherited from the cup product on the consituent Ising factors. In particular, given two basis cochains of 

TC •, ( x1 ⊗ y1) and ( x2 ⊗ y2), the cup products between these elements is given by: (x1 ⊗ y1) ⌣ (x2 ⊗ y2) = ( x1 ⌣ x2 ⊗ y1 ⌣ y2). (236) Moreover, one can readily check that the Liebniz rule for this cup product is obeyed given that the cup product on the Ising factors obeys the Leibniz rule. To get an intuitive understanding of the cup product for the toric code, we can draw out the rules implied by Eq. (236) visually (where v, e, p are product basis elements in the toric code):

This is not a cup product on the nose, as it maps TC 0 ×TC 0 → TC 1, but it nonetheless defines a cohomology action. We remark that the formula in Eq. (236) enables us to further define the cup product for any higher dimensional product of Ising models, and hence higher dimensional toric codes, as well. 49

Lemma V.4. If the cup product is input sparse in degree 1 and q for integers q between 1 and ℓ − 1and output sparse in degree 1 and q′ for integers q′

between 1 and ℓ − 2, then the gate of Eq. (244) can be implemented in constant depth. 

Proof. We start by remarking that the gate of Eq. (244), 

U ⌣

> Cℓ−1Z

[M(ℓ)], is a product of commuting Cℓ−1Z gates. As a consequence, it suffices to show that the number of gates acting on any particular qubit is O(1). Pick an arbitrary qubit at location ej in code C(j). For a Cℓ−1Z

gate containing ej to appear in Eq. (244), we must have that 

IM(ℓ) ({ei}) = 

Z

> M(ℓ)

e1 ⌣ (e2 ⌣ (· · · (eℓ−1 ⌣ eℓ))) (248) is non-zero and our goal is to show that the number of tuples ( ei) containing a fixed ej and satisfying this prop-erty is O(1). The proof proceeds by considering partial products. For j ≤ k ≤ ℓ − 1, define rk = ek ⌣ (ek+1 ⌣ (· · · eℓ)) ∈

Cℓ+1 −k and rℓ = eℓ. By output sparsity, each rk has 

O(1) support (unless k = j = 1). Indeed, this holds for rℓ

trivially, while if rk+1 has O(1) support, then rk = ek ⌣

rk+1 is a sum of O(1) cup products of basis elements, each of which has O(1) support. First, we show that there are only O(1) choices of 

ej+1 , . . . , eℓ that make IM(ℓ) ({ei})̸ = 0 with ej fixed. As IM(ℓ) ({ei})̸ = 0, we have that rj̸ = 0, and thus there is some x ∈ support ( rj+1 ) such that ej ⌣ x̸ = 0. By input sparsity, there are only O(1) possible choices for 

x. For any fixed x ∈ support ( rj+1 ), there must be some 

y ∈ support ( rj+2 ) such that 

x ∈ support ( ej+1 ⌣ y) . (249) The second condition of output sparsity gives only O(1) possible pairs ( ej+1 , y). Applying the same argument to each such y, and continuing up to rℓ = eℓ, gives only 

O(1) choices of ej+1 , . . . , eℓ.Second, we similarly show that there are only O(1) choices of ej−1, . . . , e1 that make IM(ℓ) ({ei})̸ = 0 with 

ej fixed. As rj has O(1) support, if ej−1 ⌣ rj̸ = 0, then there is some x ∈ support ( rj ) such that ej−1 ⌣

x̸ = 0. Input sparsity therefore gives only O(1) choices for ej−1. The resulting partial product again has O(1) support by output sparsity, so repeating this argument gives only O(1) choices of ej−2, . . . , e1. Thus, any qubit participates in only O(1) of the Cℓ−1Z gates appearing in Eq. (244) and hence the gate can be implemented in constant depth. We note in particular that the total product e1 ⌣ (... ⌣

eℓ) does not have to be low weight, which is why output sparsity is not required between degrees 1 and ℓ − 1. The only data from the total product that is used is binary: 51 whether or not it pairs with the cycle M(ℓ). Thus, a large total product does not result in a circuit with many gates.  

> B. Cup Products and Cohomology Invariants from the Chain Map Hierarchy

Thus far, we have discussed cup products, cohomol-ogy invariants derived from them, and the role of these invariants in defining logical gates. Given these connec-tions, numerous approaches have been developed to de-fine cup products or their cohomology invariants on the chain complexes associated with qLDPC codes. These approaches take one of two forms: 1. Full Leibniz Rule Cup Products : In some works [22, 23, 25, 26], cup products—i.e. bilinear co-chain products that satisfy the Leibniz Rule—are defined on chain complexes associated with certain qLDPC codes. These products are typically defined by ei-ther exploiting the sheaf structure naturally associ-ated with particular qLDPC codes or by mapping qLDPC codes to high-dimensional manifolds. 2. Integrated Leibniz Rule Cup Products : In other works [21, 58, 59], a pseudo-cup product is devel-oped that doesn’t satisfy the Leibniz rule. These pseudocup products satisfy a relaxation of the Leib-niz rule called the integrated Leibniz rule [c.f. dis-cussion around Eq. (243)], which enables them to define certain cohomology invariants without pro-viding a product structure on cohomology. We now show how these approaches connect to the chain map hierarchy language built up in our work. In particular, we point out that cup products that satisfy the Leibniz rule are fully parameterized by chain maps (i.e. cycles) at the third level of the chain map hierar-chy and hence product structures on cohomology are en-compassed within our formalism. Moreover, we further point out that the cohomology invariants developed from cup products or pseudo-cup products further correspond to various cycles in the chain map hierarchy. Both ob-servations follow from well-known facts in homological algebra. These observations highlight the role of the chain map hierarchy formalism in relation to prior constructions. In particular, prior work on cup products and cohomol-ogy invariants for qLDPC codes provide routes for ex-plicitly finding sparse constructions of gates on qLDPC codes that preserve their code space. Such approaches do not explicitly specify a target logical action of these cup products and do not necessarily exhaust the logical non-Clifford gates that can be present in a code. These explicit constructions also come at the cost of a more restrictive structure. As a concrete example, the con-struction of Ref. [21] has the following features: (1) the 

ℓ-cycle M(ℓ) is always a global sum of all elements of the basis B(Cℓ), (2) a ⌣ b̸ = 0 only when a and b are neighbors on the qLDPC code’s hypergraph (defined by the checks), and (3) if a ⌣ b = c, then c is close on the hypergraph to a and b.The chain map hierarchy language complements these constructions with greater generality, at the cost of spar-sity no longer being automatic. It provides an exhaustive search space in which to look for cup products and co-homology invariants, and target logical actions can be specified as part of the search. It also relaxes all the constraints on locality above. As an explicit example for classical codes, given a basis of cohomology classes [c(i)] ∈ B(H1(C)), we can pick a collection of chain maps 

φ(i) 

> •

: C• → C• indexed by B(H1(C)) to define the fol-lowing valid cup product on C•.

ap ⌣ bq =

 P  

> i

c(i) × ap(φ(i)(bq )) p + q = 1 ,

0 otherwise. (250) This escapes all the conditions mentioned in the previous paragraph: (1) integrating against distinct elements of a dual basis of cycles M(i)(1) yields distinct invariants and gates, (2) a ⌣ b can be highly non-local if φ(i) is, and (3) the image contains a fixed term ci, which can be arbi-trarily distant from the location of a and b in the hyper-graph. Despite these relaxations, this cup product can still yield a transversal gate. Indeed, these relaxations are necessary to find both the CCZ gate from Subsec-tion IV E, and the gates found in a companion work [65]. The above construction is natural in the chain map hierarchy formalism for cup products, which we now de-scribe.  

> 1. Cup Products and the Chain Map Hierarchy

To establish a connection between the cup product and the chain map hierarchy, we start by showing that ele-ments of the chain map hierarchy of C• can be used to provide a universal parameterization of the space of cup products, i.e. bilinear products that satisfy the Leibniz rule. In particular, let us start by defining the following fam-ily of cup products before proving that this family is universal. This family of cup products is defined for a chain complex C• and is parameterized by the 0-cycles of the chain map complex [ C• ⊗ C•, C •] ≃ C• ⊗ C• ⊗ C•,which crucially lives at the third level of the chain map hierarchy of C•. Specifically, for a given 0-cycle ∆ • ∈

[C• ⊗ C•, C •] with components ∆ r : ( C• ⊗ C•)r → Cr ,we can define the cochain product associated with ∆, denoted ⌣∆, as: 

ap ⌣∆ bq ≡ ∆p+q (a ⊗ b) (251) where ap ∈ Cp and bq ∈ Cq .We now prove that this cochain product defines a cup product by proving it is bilinear and satisfies the Leibniz rule. In particular, 52 

Lemma V.5. Cup Products from the Chain Map Hierarchy. Given a chain complex C• and a 0-cycle ∆ • ∈ [C• ⊗ C•, C •], the cochain product of Eq. (251) defines a cup product. Moreover, the sparsity of this cup product is re-lated to the sparsity of ∆ • when decomposed into a direct sum of maps: ∆• = M

> r

M

> p+q=r

∆p,q 

!| {z }

> ∆r

(252) where ∆ p,q : Cp ⊗ Cq → Cp+q . In particular, ⌣∆ is output sparse in degree p and q if ∆ p,q is sparse as a matrix [with rows and columns labeled by B(Cp+q )and B(Cp ⊗ Cq )]. Moreover, viewing ∆ p,q as a three-component tensor (∆ p,q )αβ γ [with α, β, γ labeling 

B(Cp), B(Cq ), and B(Cp+q ), respectively], ⌣∆ is in-put sparse if this tensor has an O(1) number of non-zero rows for fixed α and O(1) number of non-zero rows for fixed β.

Proof. Note that Eq. (251) is manifestly bilinear (due to linearity of ∆ and bilinearity of ⊗) so it suffices to show that it satisfies the Leibniz rule. To do so, let us note that: d( ap ⌣∆ bq ) = d∆ p+q (a ⊗ b) = ∆ p+q+1 (d( a ⊗ b)) = ∆ p+q+1 (a ⊗ db + d a ⊗ b)= a ⌣∆ db + d a ⌣∆ b (253) where the second equality on the first line follows from the fact that ∆ • is a chain map and hence d ◦ ∆p+q =∆p+q+1 ◦ d, the second line follows from the definition of the co-boundary map on the tensor product, and the last line follows by linearity and the definition of ⌣∆.Consequently, ⌣∆ satisfies the Leibniz rule and defines a cup product on C•.Moreover, showing that the sparsity of ⌣∆ is a conse-quence of the sparsity of ∆ immediately follows from the definition of input and output sparsity. The parameterization of Eq. (251) nicely establishes a way to define a cup product given a certain element of the chain map hierarchy. However, we now show that this parameterization is, in fact, universal —i.e. we show that any bilinear co-chain product that satisfies the Leibniz rule must take the form of Eq. (251). 

Theorem V.6. Universality of Cup Product Parameterization. Let ⌣ be a cup product defined for a chain complex C•. Then, there exists a ∆ ∈

[C• ⊗ C•, C •] such that ⌣=⌣∆.

Proof. The proof follows by applying the so-called uni-versal property of the tensor product of chain complexes: any bilinear map that satisfies the Leibniz rule factors through a chain map ∆ : C• ⊗ C• → C•. This prop-erty itself follows immediately from the usual universal property of the tensor product for vector spaces. More explicitly, let us start by noting that we can decompose the ⌣ product into a direct sum of bilinear maps: 

⌣ = M

> ⌣r

(254) where ⌣p,q : Cp × Cq → Cp+q . Now, note that since ⌣p,q 

is a bilinear map, the universal property of the tensor product for vector spaces is that any bilinear map on vector spaces must factor through the tensor product. 

Cp × Cq ⊗

(255) In other words, any bilinear map must satisfy ap ⌣p,q 

bq = ∆ p,q (ap ⊗bq ) for some ∆ p,q and any choice of inputs 

ap ∈ Cp and bq ∈ Cq . Now, note that the collection of maps ∆ • = {∆p,q } forms a 0-chain of the chain map complex of [ C• ⊗ C•, C •] by definition. Consequently, it suffices to show that ∆ • is a 0-cycle of [ C• ⊗ C•, C •]. To do so, let us recognize that since ⌣ satisfies the Leibniz rule, it is the case that: 

ap ⌣p,q +1 dbq + d ap ⌣p+1 ,q bq = d( ap ⌣p,q bq ) (256) As a consequence, we have that: d∆ p+q (a ⊗ b) = ∆ p+q+1 (d( a ⊗ b)) (257) Since this holds for all a ⊗ b, we find that d ◦ ∆p+q =∆p+q+1 ◦ d, which cements the collection of maps ∆ p,q as a 0-cycle of [ C• ⊗ C•, C •].  

> 2. Cohomology Invariants and the Chain Map Hierarchy

The above shows that the chain map hierarchy pro-vides a natural language for characterizing the space of cup products. We now show that it further encompasses cohomology invariants derived from the cup product and even those described from pseudo-cup products that sat-isfy the integrated Leibniz rule of Eq. (243).
